- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C03-4|MT038-C03-4]] — Test design should follow the essence of what the strategy captures; this may be market physicality or a mathematical trick, not necessarily a backtest.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C03-5|MT038-C03-5]] — Trend and momentum were found in the data before being explained, so let the data guide with conviction in a way that avoids overfitting.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C06-4|MT038-C06-4]] — AQR's published BAB factor has 13 years of out-of-sample data and remains strong.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C07-5|MT038-C07-5]] — The BAB paper may not control for quality as a factor; the quality explanation is untested.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C07-X1|MT038-C07-X1]] — Yuval doubts the leverage-constraint explanation for BAB because investors wanting more risk can get it through leveraged ETFs or futures rather than by overpaying for high-beta stocks.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C08-4|MT038-C08-4]] — The BAB effect is weaker before 1945 and stronger afterwards.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C09-1|MT038-C09-1]] — In the paper's model BAB's expected return is proportional to (beta high - beta low) over residual volatility, which is proportional to beta high times beta low.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C10-1|MT038-C10-1]] — Paper method: regress trend positions to split into the asset-class beta component and a residual volatility-neutral tilt.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C11-5|MT038-C11-5]] — Bond markets differ in risk-on/risk-off behaviour, so building one beta per asset class is a simplification.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C13-3|MT038-C13-3]] — The paper did not optimise the carry/trend mix (no tipping point analysis), using round numbers.
- [[MT038 - Crisis Alpha Is Not What Most Investors Think - Systematic Investor - Ep.400#^MT038-C14-3|MT038-C14-3]] — Execution quality is measured against arrival price or VWAP/TWAP.

## [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve]]
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C11-2|MT039-C11-2]] — CFM's blog finds no more dispersion among trend managers than historically; the result depends on which managers and horizon are measured.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C11-3|MT039-C11-3]] — Measured dispersion depends on the number of managers; a broader set looks more CTA-like and has a larger dispersion by design.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C12-1|MT039-C12-1]] — Chasing the best-performing trend speed or manager is not clearly valuable since autocorrelation of trend returns is doubtful beyond prolonged strong trends.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C16-1|MT039-C16-1]] — IPM, a Swedish systematic macro fund using economic data, raised most investor money, then performance stopped and it closed within a year, a caution for new strategies.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C16-2|MT039-C16-2]] — Five to ten years of history is too short to trust newer strategies given evolving markets.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C16-3|MT039-C16-3]] — Backtests flatter strategies: backtested versions of CTAs' current models would look better than real histories, so backtested papers (including replication) cannot be relied upon.

## [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4]]
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C04-4|MT040-C04-4]] — Large dispersion among CTA ETFs comes from different asset-class coverage, and buyers often do not know what is behind the product.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C06-4|MT040-C06-4]] — Many managers described as short-term trend are really volatility-expansion strategies.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C06-6|MT040-C06-6]] — Sell-side notes estimating CTA flows (e.g. buy $70bn equities if the S&P closes above a level) should be ignored because much more goes into CTA positioning.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C14-3|MT040-C14-3]] — Capital market assumptions are built either from long-term historical returns or from starting yields.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C15-3|MT040-C15-3]] — Capital market assumption exercises give radically different answers depending on assumptions (valuation mean reversion vs unchanged), so should be treated with caution.

## [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner]]
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C04-X1|MT041-C04-X1]] — The test of whether DeepSeek broke the compute-scaling paradigm is whether it reaches the next frontier (o3-level) without access to new GPUs; until then the compute-scaling thesis stands.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C20-3|MT041-C20-3]] — Successful sovereign funds (Norway, Korea, Canada) have independent, consistent management, so a US fund needs the same independence.

## [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385]]
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C07-3|MT042-C07-3]] — Economic trend (growth acceleration, inflation change) and price trend are 40-50% correlated and differ in timing, so they complement each other.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C08-3|MT042-C08-3]] — CTA design choices (speed, market count, risk allocation, vol sizing, position sizing) are manager-specific and have no single right answer.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C08-4|MT042-C08-4]] — Managers' returns correlate highly yet design choices (market set, speed) cause large return gaps in extreme years, and the best choice flips from year to year.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C08-X1|MT042-C08-X1]] — Economic-trend models run on global data from many regions plus unofficial sources, so a gap in official US releases (the shutdown) only partly blinds them.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C10-1|MT042-C10-1]] — Weekly rebalancing (or delayed execution) cut differences in April 2025, so speed also depends on trade frequency and signal delays.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C10-2|MT042-C10-2]] — Some managers delay trading 3 days after a signal, an execution-speed lever separate from lookback length.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C10-3|MT042-C10-3]] — Speed combines window, trade adjustment and signal smoothing; nonlinear or ML models can have time-varying speed.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C10-4|MT042-C10-4]] — In 25 years of data the best lookback in about 70% of years was 200+ days, but some runs of years differ (56 days best in 2008); Nick Bolters's analysis agrees.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C11-3|MT042-C11-3]] — Data preprocessing before signals is an important, undisclosed part of CTA edge.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C12-1|MT042-C12-1]] — The 10-asset trend portfolio underperformed a diversified basket for 10 years straight, so a concentrated set can be right for years or wrong for a decade; replication must be diversified.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C16-2|MT042-C16-2]] — Geopolitical risk can be measured with LLM or sentiment indicators from news data, with a UC Irvine paper on geopolitical risk and stock returns.

## [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20]]
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C02-1|MT043-C02-1]] — The fund is discretionary and bottom-up: supply and demand analysis for each commodity, with optimisation and timing tools only for execution.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C03-2|MT043-C03-2]] — The universe is oil, gas, industrial metals and softs, excluding precious metals, which are not driven by physical supply and demand.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C05-1|MT043-C05-1]] — Tracking ships and tank tops is too granular; deep supply-demand work and demand stress tests matter more.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C05-3|MT043-C05-3]] — The fund took physical delivery (rubber, raw sugar) to check whether scarcity was real before staying bullish.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C11-2|MT043-C11-2]] — Information comes from physical market participants, not from derivative sellers.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C11-3|MT043-C11-3]] — Edge in trading is filtering relevant from irrelevant information.

## [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor]]
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C03-3|MT044-C03-3]] — Rob's current drawdown is his largest live and about equal to his worst backtest drawdowns since 1970, a one-in-10 to one-in-20 year event.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C04-2|MT044-C04-2]] — Expressing P&L as an annualised Sharpe ratio gives intuition for drawdowns: Rob is at -1.3 Sharpe units for 2025 against a long-run Sharpe of 0.5-1.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C05-3|MT044-C05-3]] — Rob builds an asset-class index equally weighted by volatility (US weight about 10% not 70% as in a cap-weighted index) to act as a factor.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C06-2|MT044-C06-2]] — PCA of crypto shows 70% weight on Bitcoin; in equities PCA and cap weights give similar index; other asset classes are less clear.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C06-3|MT044-C06-3]] — Metals have two drivers: precious metals as a risk-off factor and industrial metals tied to growth, so a PCA would likely give two factors.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C07-3|MT044-C07-3]] — Index plus residual trend gives results almost identical to plain trend following; about 70% of profit comes from trending the asset-class index and 30% from relative trends.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C08-1|MT044-C08-1]] — A limited-capital investor can trade only the main instrument per asset class (S&P, 5-10 year bond, gold or metals) and get about 70% of the performance.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C08-2|MT044-C08-2]] — Removing more principal components could leave a mean-reverting residual that supports a mean-reversion strategy.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C08-3|MT044-C08-3]] — A PCA across the whole futures universe could be used as a trading strategy, not just a risk tool.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C08-4|MT044-C08-4]] — The replication argument says a few factors explain CTA performance, so those can be traded alone; Rob notes this is PCA of trend returns not of markets.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C11-4|MT044-C11-4]] — Rob scales every signal into a forecast from -20 to +20 (10 = average bullish) so systematic and discretionary views can be combined.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C11-X1|MT044-C11-X1]] — Black-Litterman is the classic framework for blending discretionary return views into a systematic (e.g. market-cap) portfolio; Carver's simpler alternative is a weighted average of scaled forecasts.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C12-1|MT044-C12-1]] — Discretionary forecasts on the same scale can be blended with systematic ones using weights (50/50, 70/30), which Rob calls semi-automatic trading.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C12-2|MT044-C12-2]] — A numeric forecast can be tested like a backtest, so the weight on discretionary views can be based on evidence.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C12-3|MT044-C12-3]] — Do not apply discretionary views to volatility forecasts because systematic forecasts are hard to beat.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C12-4|MT044-C12-4]] — Only the buy-or-not decision may be discretionary; sizing, holding period, stops and portfolio optimisation should be systematic, as done by Marshall Wace feeding forecasts into an optimiser.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C14-2|MT044-C14-2]] — Skilled managers differ in small details such as beta cleaning; there is no big undiscovered secret, only marginal gains in execution and data cleaning.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C14-X1|MT044-C14-X1]] — Return differences between CTAs are largely luck, so it is hard to tell skilled from unskilled managers from outcomes; post-mortems should ask whether a loss came from bad luck or a bad decision such as overweighting an illiquid market.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C18-3|MT044-C18-3]] — Ask who is on the other side of a risk premium trade (Ilmanen): knowing the counterparty helps judge whether a factor persists.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C19-1|MT044-C19-1]] — Avoiding the worst days beats missing the best days because equity returns are negatively skewed, so Asness's critique of the 'missing the best days' argument holds.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C19-2|MT044-C19-2]] — Trend-based exposure changes are different from forecasting one-day moves; nobody sits out single days.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C19-3|MT044-C19-3]] — Simulations of randomly missing days raise dispersion of returns, reinforcing the case against day-timing; costs and taxes add to the damage.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C20-2|MT044-C20-2]] — Timing entry into trend following needs autocorrelation of strategy returns: positive would mean add after good years, negative the reverse.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C21-1|MT044-C21-1]] — Performance fees bias net returns toward negative autocorrelation, so autocorrelation tests should use gross returns.
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C21-3|MT044-C21-3]] — The autocorrelation effects are too weak to time trend following; strategy timing is as hard as market timing.

## [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356]]
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C05-1|MT045-C05-1]] — Among managed-futures drawdowns above 10%, the largest was 2015-2019 (Trump's first term era), and the paper plots drawdown depth, duration and recovery.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C09-1|MT045-C09-1]] — The Man paper shows rolling 12-month CTA returns below -5% are very rare compared to returns above +15% (about 59 vs 2-4 instances).
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C10-4|MT045-C10-4]] — CFM's work separates exogenous shocks (Liberation Day tariffs) from endogenous ones (2022 rates positioning) for CTA performance.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C11-X1|MT045-C11-X1]] — Turbulence metrics (magnitude-of-surprise measures across assets) can be used to identify and size exogenous shocks when studying their effect on trend following.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C12-3|MT045-C12-3]] — A non-parametric regime approach avoids pre-defining regimes (rising or falling rates), which creates bias; critics say the seven state variables were chosen with hindsight.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C13-1|MT045-C13-1]] — The Harvey et al. regime method standardises seven state variables (equities, yield curve shape, volatility, copper, etc.) and measures Euclidean distance of the current month to every past month.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C14-1|MT045-C14-1]] — Rule-based regime definitions overfit history, so tests must be walk-forward and account for how many variables were tried.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C14-3|MT045-C14-3]] — Replicators outperformed their benchmarks in 2025, implying either large tracking error or a separate alpha strategy.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C15-2|MT045-C15-2]] — Replicator tracking error in 2025 is within expected range per the paper's methodology.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C15-3|MT045-C15-3]] — Regression-based replication is slowest; mechanical or hybrid replication can be faster to track CTA performance.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C16-2|MT045-C16-2]] — Tracking error depends on market set and implementation, so investors must understand inputs to measure it.

## [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio]]
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C05-3|MT046-C05-3]] — His shorting process projects the situation 12-18 months ahead and shorts securities he expects to be lower, using the same approach as for longs.

## [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340]]
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C08-2|MT047-C08-2]] — Long-run return databases such as the Global Investment Returns Yearbook provide anchors for long-term asset return expectations.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C12-X1|MT047-C12-X1]] — Five-year look-backs are a useful complement to century-long data because they are recent enough to remember the starting consensus, showing how fast the macro regime can change (secular stagnation in 2019 to 4.2% inflation in 2020-24).
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C14-4|MT047-C14-4]] — Quantica estimates trend following on all 69 commodity markets without capacity limits has twice the expected Sharpe of the same strategy on only the top 10 most liquid markets.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C17-2|MT047-C17-2]] — Portfolio Sharpe depends on the average Sharpe and average correlation: 20 markets with Sharpe 0.2 and correlation 0.05 yield about 0.64 (transcript says 64), potentially better than 100 more correlated markets.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C17-X1|MT047-C17-X1]] — Manager research on design questions (number of markets, capacity) tends to support the author's own business model, so findings should be weighed against the sponsor's incentives.

## [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C04-2|MT049-C04-2]] — Before subscribing, Prince checked the research firm's past published reports for accuracy, a simple track-record verification of a forecaster.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C05-1|MT049-C05-1]] — Bridgewater's edge is integrating economic understanding with market understanding (flows, risk, reward) into one system.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C05-2|MT049-C05-2]] — Systemising a view by coding it into rules and then trading it forces accountability and honest evaluation of outcomes, instead of adjusting the story after the fact.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C07-3|MT049-C07-3]] — To answer the long-duration question the team assembled bond-yield and yield-curve data back to the 1860s, producing a landmark study; long history is key to testing deflation hedges.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C08-1|MT049-C08-1]] — Derive answers from the basic mechanics of how the world works, independent of what others do, because convention settles in.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C08-3|MT049-C08-3]] — Risk comes from what you do not know you are missing; stating humility explicitly and combining people with different capabilities provides a window into blind spots.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C12-2|MT049-C12-2]] — Bridgewater's systematic fundamental process avoids a single decision-maker; research and plans are made in groups as a team effort.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C13-1|MT049-C13-1]] — Compounding a small difference in annual rate (10% vs 5%) is trivial at 10 years but sixfold at 40 years, so long horizons magnify skill differences; play the long game.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C13-2|MT049-C13-2]] — Economics textbooks rest on false assumptions and no book explains how the world works, so one must ask own questions and find own answers from data, books and people.

## [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting]]
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C05-4|MT050-C05-4]] — The process runs top-down: politics, then business cycle, then implications for rates, currencies and capital flows, and then bonds, equities, defensive vs deep cyclical sectors, currency hedging and commodities.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C06-3|MT050-C06-3]] — Every weekend he reviews about 1,000 charts (money market rates, bond yields, 30 currencies, equity and sector indices, the top 100 stocks in US, Europe and Asia) on long, medium and short horizons, for about five hours.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C06-4|MT050-C06-4]] — Fundamentals set the business-cycle view while technical analysis checks whether the market confirms it and shows the health of the trend.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C06-5|MT050-C06-5]] — Where price action disagrees with the thesis, he digs deeper for what is wrong; the market's disagreement is a signal to re-examine.
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C07-1|MT050-C07-1]] — He scans news for items that do not fit his thesis and checks them, treating the thesis like a scientific hypothesis to revise when contradicted.

## [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management]]
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C05-2|MT051-C05-2]] — Learning comes from failures: after a loss, look back and work out why, as the Nifty Fifty lesson taught him that price matters.
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C08-2|MT051-C08-2]] — Knowledge is drawing right inferences from a modest number of facts; the challenge is filtering information, not ingesting it all.
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C08-X1|MT051-C08-X1]] — Reading sources with opposing slants (NYT and WSJ, their op-ed pages) gives a fuller picture than a single viewpoint when forming market judgements.
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C09-X1|MT051-C09-X1]] — Which asset class will be most profitable a decade ahead cannot be forecast, so long-horizon asset-class picks should not be based on such predictions.

## [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates#^MT053-C04-1|MT053-C04-1]] — Successful investors understand the causal drivers of markets rather than following what worked in the past.
- [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates#^MT053-C04-2|MT053-C04-2]] — Investors should challenge their own beliefs and, when a strategy shows a high Sharpe ratio, ask why it works and whether it will continue as in the past.
- [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates#^MT053-C04-X1|MT053-C04-X1]] — Investment research is the same analytical process as consulting or engineering: break a new problem into pieces, build a framework, then find data to test it.

## [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann]]
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C02-X1|MT055-C02-X1]] — Consumer surplus of a company can be estimated by valuing the hours it saves consumers and capitalising that at about 20x; for Amazon this gives roughly $10 trillion, at least five times its market value.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C05-3|MT055-C05-3]] — In choosing causes, the value is the size of the benefit times the probability it can be achieved, so a smaller idea with high chance beats a bigger one with near zero chance.

## [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395]]
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C07-X1|MT056-C07-X1]] — Bloomberg's hedge-fund return rankings (published by Nishant Kumar) are a timely data source for checking whether CTA drawdowns are shared by the wider macro hedge-fund space.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C08-2|MT056-C08-2]] — Over the last five years the average CTA ETF returned about 6.1% a year with a higher Sharpe ratio than the SG CTA index and outperformed mutual funds and UCITS, so more complexity does not produce higher alpha.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C08-X1|MT056-C08-X1]] — Morningstar data: 16 managed-futures ETFs (from about 2 in 2019), 18 US mutual funds and 45 UCITS funds, with UCITS AUM below mutual-fund AUM; this population allows wrapper-by-wrapper performance comparisons, with short histories as a caveat.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C09-1|MT056-C09-1]] — ETF outperformance may be selection bias from a few funds launching and doing well; Beer says the evidence has not yet shown that and will re-check periodically.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C10-4|MT056-C10-4]] — Equity-index signals from many markets can be aggregated and implemented in fewer positions, so signal complexity does not require implementation complexity.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C10-5|MT056-C10-5]] — The replication model launched July 2016 outperformed each of the 20 SG CTA index constituents of that time on absolute and risk-adjusted return, supporting efficiency over complexity.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C11-1|MT056-C11-1]] — Claimed excess return of 400 bp a year over the average SG CTA index constituent since July 2016.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C11-5|MT056-C11-5]] — Niels also doubts that trading hundreds of markets is better, though some peers do well with hundreds of markets due to exposure to different factors.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C12-1|MT056-C12-1]] — Comparison to constituents of a fixed starting set is biased because later managers that did better were added to the index, so index composition changes over time (survivorship/inclusion).
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C12-2|MT056-C12-2]] — Beer could not beat the SG CTA index via own models, QIS products or manager selection, so he sought a structural efficiency advantage in capturing the same signal.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C12-3|MT056-C12-3]] — The replication beats the index in 54% of one-week rolling windows (overshoot +73 bp, undershoot -70 bp), which compounds to about 80% over a year and 90%+ over three years.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C12-X1|MT056-C12-X1]] — An edge over a benchmark comes either from a higher hit rate or from positive skew of wins; replication's edge is a small hit-rate advantage with symmetric outcomes, not skew.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C13-1|MT056-C13-1]] — Five to ten years of performance data is limited evidence about the future of a product.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C13-2|MT056-C13-2]] — Hedge funds cannot easily stop publishing; mutual fund and UCITS disclosure forces data, and a US multi-manager CTA mutual fund is the cleanest signal source.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C13-X1|MT056-C13-X1]] — Replication inputs need not be hedge-fund returns only: QIS product returns or the firm's own internal trend models (used for risk management, not invested) can also supply the signal.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C15-1|MT056-C15-1]] — Trend following is not an identifiable beta: different trend models or QIS products can differ by 20-30 points, unlike US large-cap equity indices.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C15-2|MT056-C15-2]] — Merger arbitrage replication also fails as a single beta: once parameters are specified, portfolios concentrate in different deals and diverge.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C15-X1|MT056-C15-X1]] — Factor-based replication works poorly for low-net strategies such as merger arbitrage, which is why a bolt-on QIS product was considered instead.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C16-1|MT056-C16-1]] — Bank QIS products sold with long track records (e.g. G10 carry Sharpe 1.4 in backtest) show Sharpe near 0.2 live, as the track record is mostly a backtest.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C16-2|MT056-C16-2]] — QIS products are closed and replaced, so failing strategies disappear and surviving ones look better; this survivorship makes allocators unable to evaluate them.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C16-3|MT056-C16-3]] — Index backtests are what the bank wishes it had traded, with dials tuned, so they are weak evidence of future results.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C16-X2|MT056-C16-X2]] — QIS strategies come in multiple versions (different vol targets, excess vs total return), so allocators must normalise for volatility and cash return before comparing products.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C17-1|MT056-C17-1]] — About 17 tracked QIS trend products have returned roughly 200 bp a year less than the SG CTA index.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C17-2|MT056-C17-2]] — QIS trend products lose about 90% of their Sharpe ratio between in-sample backtest and out-of-sample live.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C17-4|MT056-C17-4]] — Indices are not supposed to change after launch, so banks launch new indices for each enhancement, producing a constant stream of products without live history.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C17-X1|MT056-C17-X1]] — Annual return dispersion among QIS trend products reaches up to 40 percentage points in some years, so the choice of product dominates any notion of a common trend beta.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C17-X2|MT056-C17-X2]] — Niels contrasts fixed QIS indices with CTAs that keep researching and upgrading models, arguing ongoing research improves a trend product over time.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C17-X3|MT056-C17-X3]] — Premialab aggregates bank QIS index data and is a source for allocators trying to compare QIS products.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C18-X1|MT056-C18-X1]] — Swap-based products on a manager's index report only the wrapper fee, not the underlying index fee, so reported expense ratios understate total cost.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C19-X1|MT056-C19-X1]] — Fee reporting can be gamed (e.g. 0% management / 40% incentive share classes reported as zero fee), so only net returns allow fair comparison.

## [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300]]
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C04-1|MT057-C04-1]] — Backtest first for trend suitability, then add actual broker costs, which may eliminate some markets or models as untradeable.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C05-1|MT057-C05-1]] — Use an LLM to read and explain academic quant papers to lay readers step by step.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C06-1|MT057-C06-1]] — The 2023 paper (Optimal trend following rules in two-state switching regime models) says trend followers build models by ad hoc heavy optimization and proposes mathematical formulas (Markov processes, autoregression) to derive optimal models.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C06-2|MT057-C06-2]] — The mathematically optimal models are simple, medium-to-long-term holds with loose parameter sets, matching those found through practitioner backtesting.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C06-3|MT057-C06-3]] — Stark applied the equity-derived optimal parameters to futures on unseen data and got a Sharpe ratio of about 1.2 over 20-30 years.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C06-X1|MT057-C06-X1]] — The paper compares three trend-rule families - moving-average crossovers (simple, linearly weighted, exponentially weighted), MACD and momentum - where linear and exponential weights put more weight on recent prices.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C06-X2|MT057-C06-X2]] — An analytically derived optimal rule can be ported quickly to new markets without fresh brute-force optimisation, which also reduces data-mining risk relative to market-by-market backtesting.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C07-1|MT057-C07-1]] — A Markov model infers a chosen number of market states from the data via Bayesian inference, giving the probability of being in each state and a transition matrix, which may not match bull/bear labels.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C08-1|MT057-C08-1]] — In a hidden Markov model, trend-model returns act as thermometers of the market state, giving state-dependent weights per model and a short-term forecast of the next state.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C09-1|MT057-C09-1]] — A semi-Markov model lets transition probabilities change with trend duration, giving both model allocation and when to switch between models.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C09-2|MT057-C09-2]] — Autoregression (serial correlation across many lags) and Markov models are mathematically equivalent views of the same trend persistence, so moving-average, EMA, MACD and momentum rules can be optimized analytically.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C09-3|MT057-C09-3]] — Under a plain Markov two-state model the exponential moving average was the best trend rule.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C10-1|MT057-C10-1]] — Under the more realistic semi-Markov model MACD was the best rule, with optimal parameters matching what practitioners find.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C10-3|MT057-C10-3]] — Regime prediction is only reliable over a very short horizon (days to a week), like weather forecasts out to about 14 days, and decays quickly afterwards.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C11-X1|MT057-C11-X1]] — Niels cautions that analytically 'optimal' parameters cannot be a holy grail; if everyone could derive them the edge would not hold, so the result supports but does not settle parameter choice.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C12-2|MT057-C12-2]] — Niels questions why the same technology did not beat benchmarks for Renaissance external funds.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C12-X1|MT057-C12-X1]] — The Baum-Welch algorithm (Leonard Baum, Renaissance) is the estimation method needed to fit hidden Markov regime models, which Renaissance used from its code-breaking origins.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C13-X1|MT057-C13-X1]] — Rich doubts that Markov/AI forecasting methods work at medium-to-long horizons because uncertainty compounds, so their edge is confined to the short term.

## [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C03-1|MT058-C03-1]] — Treat losses as signals: reflect on how reality works and codify a principle, which turns mistakes into improved rules.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C04-3|MT058-C04-3]] — Stress test views with the smartest people who disagree, since confidence without challenge is the main source of decision error.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C05-1|MT058-C05-1]] — Surprises come from events not seen in a lifetime but common in history, so study history whenever something big and new happens.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C05-2|MT058-C05-2]] — Studying the 1930s let Dalio anticipate the 2008 crisis and Bridgewater made money when most lost.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C12-2|MT058-C12-2]] — Write explicit decision rules, back-test them on history (e.g. the 1930s), then put them into algorithms; rules learned from the 1930s made money in 2008.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C14-1|MT058-C14-1]] — Weight votes by data-measured believability in each domain, creating an idea meritocracy rather than relying on authority.

## [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming]]
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C02-1|MT059-C02-1]] — LLMs help digest thousands of pages of market rules and train juniors but hallucinate and can be talked into agreeing with a wrong position, so a domain expert must correct them.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C03-2|MT059-C03-2]] — Equities are hard to find edge in because information (supply chains, customers) is public to everyone, whereas power has dispersed, hard-to-find information.

## [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit]]
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C02-4|MT061-C02-4]] — In handicapping special situations you only need roughly 55% of outcomes right to win on Wall Street, so deep analysis of players and incentives pays.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C03-2|MT061-C03-2]] — Before committing to a contrarian macro thesis he sought out the Fed and academics such as Rogoff and asked them to refute it.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C03-X1|MT061-C03-X1]] — Economists routinely drop Japan from cross-country averages because it is an outlier; he found his trade by studying exactly the case everyone excluded and asking how it could survive 200% debt/GDP.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C06-5|MT061-C06-5]] — He built an edge on China by studying how its closed-capital-account banking system and RMB/CNH link work.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C07-1|MT061-C07-1]] — Sell-side research was selling Chinese stocks and VIE structures while nobody mapped the banking architecture, an information gap he exploited.

## [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin]]
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C01-X1|MT062-C01-X1]] — CFM runs a flat research organisation with no pods: all researchers work on one shared research base whose output propagates to every strategy, so ideas are tested once against a common predictor set rather than inside siloed books.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C01-X2|MT062-C01-X2]] — Graham runs roughly 70-75% of its assets as quantitative macro and about 25% as discretionary PMs and treats the two as different methods applied to the same markets and the same problems, not as different businesses.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C03-2|MT062-C03-2]] — Quant macro can use many correlated inputs because machine learning compresses data, and uses nonlinear pattern-recognition regressions plus robust portfolio construction.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C03-3|MT062-C03-3]] — A systematic program has to be rigid, since inserting news (a weekend AI release, tariff comments) manually removes alpha.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C04-3|MT062-C04-3]] — Systematic strategies search a large idea space and hold hundreds of competing ideas, giving more diversity than discretionary books.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C05-5|MT062-C05-5]] — Manager selection underwrites people and process: require a tested, repeatable process and a proven track record, and do not pay for someone's tuition.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C05-6|MT062-C05-6]] — Systematic managers differ on strategy mix, trading speed, holding period and asset class mix, so outcomes vary by manager.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C05-X2|MT062-C05-X2]] — In manager selection the allocator prefers specialists whose expertise is narrow but very deep and proven, over generalists.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-3|MT062-C06-3]] — New macro predictors are rejected if they correlate with existing signals, often a cluster of trend following predictors, because they add no new information.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-4|MT062-C06-4]] — Systematic research looks for effects statistically inconsistent with luck.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-5|MT062-C06-5]] — Discretionary traders are measured only on P&L, whereas systematic firms can attribute returns to each embedded idea.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C06-6|MT062-C06-6]] — Overriding a systematic model makes it discretionary, so labels need discipline.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-3|MT062-C07-3]] — More big data and ML reduces interpretability, so humans must still understand how the system is trading.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-5|MT062-C07-5]] — Unsupervised ML overfits (strong in sample, poor out of sample), so use ML for alpha discovery and then understand why the alpha exists.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-6|MT062-C07-6]] — AI is a great tool for discovering alpha, which humans then explain.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-X2|MT062-C07-X2]] — Singular value decomposition, data compression, pattern matching and cosine similarity are old techniques; what is new is that AI now combines them into one coherent pipeline for extracting signals from large data.

## [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324]]
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C03-3|MT063-C03-3]] — Long-term performance, not 1-3 year returns, is the best measure of managing risk and uncertainty across regimes, and trend following offers protection against uncertainty.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C05-3|MT063-C05-3]] — Despite common outliers there was large dispersion among trend followers in 2024, possibly due to investment universe or strategy design.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C05-X2|MT063-C05-X2]] — Through November 2024 the short-term traders CTA index was down about 0.6% for the year while the BTOP50 was up about 3.3% and the SG Trend index about 1%, evidence of wide dispersion by style and horizon even in a year with big outliers.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C06-1|MT063-C06-1]] — Meteorology replaced single-path deterministic forecasts with ensemble forecasts, giving probability cones for hurricanes.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C06-2|MT063-C06-2]] — The 1987 Michael Fish forecast failure illustrates the danger of confident single-path forecasts based on history.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C07-1|MT063-C07-1]] — Run many simulations with slightly varied initial conditions and take probability as the share of models agreeing on an outcome.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C08-X1|MT063-C08-X1]] — Adding more distinct model types to a trend following book, like adding more markets, probably has a point of diminishing returns.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C08-X2|MT063-C08-X2]] — Which single model type a trend follower picks may be partly a luck of the draw depending on the environment, which is an argument for holding several model types.

## [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD]]
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C03-X1|MT064-C03-X1]] — Net-of-fee return is the most important figure for an investor, which is why high pass-through fees are tolerated while net results stay good.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C04-X1|MT064-C04-X1]] — Even in UCITS funds the stated expense ratio can omit extra costs such as research costs that only show up in the detailed documents, so headline ratios may understate what investors pay.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C04-X2|MT064-C04-X2]] — A 1% year-to-date result after seven weeks (down 1.5% in January, up 2.5% in February) is statistical noise, so short-window performance says nothing about a system.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C07-X1|MT064-C07-X1]] — A second trend system is highly correlated with the first because there are only so many ways to do trend following, even at different speeds, which is why new markets diversify more than new trend variants.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C08-1|MT064-C08-1]] — Adding many signals with tiny marginal gains is not worth the complexity.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C08-2|MT064-C08-2]] — Back test with the instrument you will trade: futures for futures, ETFs for ETFs (test what you trade, trade what you test).
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C08-X1|MT064-C08-X1]] — To compare an ETF with futures, estimate your own annual roll cost and compare it with the ETF's total annual expense ratio, checking that the ratio includes all costs.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C09-1|MT064-C09-1]] — Compare ETF and futures on total return: ETF includes dividends/interest less costs; futures back-adjusted prices need the risk-free rate added because margin earns interest.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C09-3|MT064-C09-3]] — A signal (feature) is turned into a forecast by scaling it, like a z-score, so it says how big the forecast is.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C10-1|MT064-C10-1]] — Regress volatility-normalized returns on the signal: the coefficient equals the forecast scaling, so regression and scaling are the same thing.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C10-2|MT064-C10-2]] — The regression intercept removes systematic long or short bias, which he prefers not to remove.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C10-3|MT064-C10-3]] — The target of a forecast should be risk-adjusted returns because positions are sized by risk.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C10-4|MT064-C10-4]] — Some CTAs run unchanged models for years and it can still be a valid approach.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C10-5|MT064-C10-5]] — Research at big CTAs is often adding new markets (such as AHL Evolution), which can give the biggest return for effort.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-3|MT064-C11-3]] — Neural networks and ML on existing data are less likely to add value; he is wary of teams focused exclusively on them (Renaissance excepted).
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-4|MT064-C11-4]] — Few new ways of doing trend following exist, but combining more than one approach (e.g. moving average crossover plus another) may give better results.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-X2|MT064-C11-X2]] — Besides new data sources, firms are researching how to pre-process data before it enters the trading algorithms, which is an active frontier of improvement.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C12-4|MT064-C12-4]] — Rob's five-year relative mean reversion strategy has a negative Sharpe but improves a baseline trend-plus-carry portfolio.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C12-X1|MT064-C12-X1]] — A systematic trader can attribute every trade's result exactly because all trades are in a database, whereas discretionary calls are rarely recorded, so their quality is hard to judge afterwards.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C13-2|MT064-C13-2]] — Long-term signals are hard to show significant and may show a negative Sharpe in a back test.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C13-3|MT064-C13-3]] — Prefer the simplest version of a strategy (Occam's razor).
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C14-2|MT064-C14-2]] — Moving averages and EMAs are weighting functions of past returns (flat or exponential).
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C14-3|MT064-C14-3]] — Nonlinear responses to recent observations add parameters and risk overfitting.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C14-4|MT064-C14-4]] — A separate short-horizon mean-reversion signal added tiny insignificant performance and was not worth the complexity.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C15-1|MT064-C15-1]] — When contracts are delisted and relisted, the simplest approach is to ignore data before relisting.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C15-2|MT064-C15-2]] — Better is a trading system that handles missing data by going flat until enough prices exist to form forecasts and volatility.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C15-3|MT064-C15-3]] — Do not interpolate missing prices in back tests; 99.9% of the time it is wrong.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C15-4|MT064-C15-4]] — Interpolating is reasonable only for volatility estimation across overnight hours, using a Brownian bridge rather than cointegration/ECM.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C15-X1|MT064-C15-X1]] — A chatbot's suggestion to fill missing contract data with cointegration or error-correction models is the kind of plausible but unsound advice he distrusts, so AI-suggested data repairs need expert review before use in a backtest.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C17-3|MT064-C17-3]] — The geometric mean return (CAGR) maximizes terminal wealth and should be the key metric.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C17-4|MT064-C17-4]] — Sharpe ratio is valid only when compared strategies have the same skew.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C18-1|MT064-C18-1]] — Simulating terminal wealth for different Sharpe and skew combinations shows the benefit of higher Sharpe outweighs lost upside volatility, so Sharpe can be optimised despite penalising upside volatility.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C19-5|MT064-C19-5]] — The paper fits a cubic polynomial across a very wide range of time frequencies to classify each horizon as momentum or mean reversion.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C20-3|MT064-C20-3]] — The paper uses data from 1692, about 330 years, and finds mean reversion out to long horizons.

## [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel]]
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C02-4|MT067-C02-4]] — Process: the economist team provides research and flags where Fed thinking differs from consensus, but portfolio managers express it independently.

## [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem]]
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C02-3|MT068-C02-3]] — Look beneath index averages (e.g. S&P ex-AI names, gold) to see shifts the headline averages hide.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C02-4|MT068-C02-4]] — Bridgewater's method: write down beliefs, stress-test them, and encode human intuition into algorithms (an expert system).
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C02-5|MT068-C02-5]] — Bridgewater built an AI agent investor (started 2022, producing alpha by 2024) run as a separate entity to generate independent ideas; machines learning rules rather than copying human intuition.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C12-1|MT068-C12-1]] — Bridgewater's edge: understand the global financial system deeply and build portfolios, by writing down beliefs and compounding knowledge in human- and machine-readable form with a record of every change.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C12-2|MT068-C12-2]] — Enforcement rule: research counts only when translated into compounded knowledge (plain-English reasoning plus code).
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C13-1|MT068-C13-1]] — One common platform for all research in place of mixed tools makes research replicable and lets AI audit the rules.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C14-1|MT068-C14-1]] — AI venture run separately, designed around AI rather than humans, to avoid copying human flaws and get an uncorrelated alpha source; now being merged back.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C14-X1|MT068-C14-X1]] — Research method: once accumulated understanding is fully systematised, the human research team (a couple hundred people) can concentrate only on what the systematic process does not yet capture, such as new regimes like modern mercantilism.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C15-2|MT068-C15-2]] — COVID lesson: trying to systemise a unique shock made the model slow; Bridgewater now accelerates new research into positions before it is fully systematized and gives researchers independence (a controlled override).
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C15-3|MT068-C15-3]] — Overruling the system helps barely on average, so it is done rarely and with discipline.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C15-X1|MT068-C15-X1]] — Caveat on historical analogies: Bridgewater's systematic COVID response leaned on the Spanish flu precedent even though they knew its economic impact differed, so a known-imperfect analogue embedded in process can override better current knowledge.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C20-1|MT068-C20-1]] — Hardest decision: moving from CIO-centralised decisions to independent investors, requiring removal of long-tenured people from the process.

## [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns]]
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C01-3|MT070-C01-3]] — Uses high-frequency data to measure correlation changes and emergence of new factors even though trading is long term; computing in the cloud allowed portfolios of 7000 stocks (from ~1000-1500 five years ago), now a third to a half of weight.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C04-1|MT070-C04-1]] — Grain price data go back 1,200 years; researching weather changes and long time series could underpin an ag trading system (not yet found).
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C04-2|MT070-C04-2]] — Government economic statistics are revised heavily (US 2009 double dip erased nine years later; Japan growth 0.4 vs 2.5), so real-time fundamental data are untrustworthy for systematic macro.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C04-3|MT070-C04-3]] — A long-run study of the PMI as a forecaster of the economy for betting on stock industries had too little data to prove anything after two years and millions in cost, and sunk cost should not force a result.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C05-2|MT070-C05-2]] — AI view: deep learning has made progress but is not general intelligence; there is 1 part reality and 9 parts hype; responsibility for algorithms must stay with named humans.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C06-2|MT070-C06-2]] — New strategies are statistical like trend following: they cannot be forecast to work in a given period, so diversification and a long horizon matter; judge managers over many years not months.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C08-2|MT070-C08-2]] — Renaissance's edge is cumulative scientific research by a large team over ~20 years, not a single data item or magic number.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C09-1|MT070-C09-1]] — Volatility is not rising: measured in percent, 1933-34 was the highest; point-based charts mislead, so use log charts.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C10-2|MT070-C10-2]] — Spun off the data-cleaning business (Hivemind) because clean data are the unglamorous prerequisite for research and AI training.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C11-1|MT070-C11-1]] — Cloud computing lets him rent huge compute for a short time for large experiments instead of owning machines, making research more efficient.

## [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408]]
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C02-1|MT071-C02-1]] — Beyond a certain level a working quant model is very hard to improve by adding enhancements, so ten years of tinkering added nothing.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C02-2|MT071-C02-2]] — A factor (the neglect factor, stocks without analyst coverage) with strong research results failed for years after live implementation, showing in-sample research often does not survive going live.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C09-3|MT071-C09-3]] — Changes to published economic data would break models built on those series, so data-definition risk matters for systematic models.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C10-6|MT071-C10-6]] — Portfolio construction should target behavioral exposures (e.g., trend, carry) rather than asset-class lists.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C14-5|MT071-C14-5]] — Managers can replicate the CFM result by running one model separately on small-tick and large-tick contracts.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C16-3|MT071-C16-3]] — Liquidity alone did not explain the cross-section; contract characteristics plus liquidity together do.

## [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350]]
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C13-3|MT072-C13-3]] — Asset allocation inputs should not be anchored to a single period such as 1980-2020, when disinflation and falling yields favored financial assets.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C13-4|MT072-C13-4]] — Scenario-based portfolio construction is better than plugging in historical averages when the regime is shifting.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C14-3|MT072-C14-3]] — The Global Investment Returns Yearbook warned in 2000 of lower returns (correct) and less US outperformance (wrong), so valuation-based forecasts have limits.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C15-3|MT072-C15-3]] — Forward-looking return estimates (dividend yield plus earnings growth, about 5-6% for US equities) correlate positively with realized 10-year returns, whereas trailing past returns correlate negatively.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C17-4|MT072-C17-4]] — The current trend following drawdown looks normal in the drawdown profile; blaming Trump is no excuse.

## [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362]]
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C05-4|MT073-C05-4]] — Most managers trade spreads for mean reversion/carry, not breakout trend.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C08-2|MT073-C08-2]] — Remove the smallest factors and overweight middle factors in a PCA-based trend system.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C08-X1|MT073-C08-X1]] — Trading costs for slow outright trend are small, roughly 0.02-0.03 of Sharpe ratio, and slow trend is the dominant source of trend returns, which sets the cost benchmark that spread trading must beat.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C10-1|MT073-C10-1]] — Test crowding by regressing speculators' COT positions on trend signals; high beta would signal crowded trend.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C11-1|MT073-C11-1]] — A Bank of England-led paper (Topography of the FX derivative market) uses regulator-level non-anonymized London FX derivative data for 2015-2020.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C11-2|MT073-C11-2]] — Describe data structure first before building predictive strategies; avoid overcomplicated models.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C11-3|MT073-C11-3]] — Understand the data before using machine learning on it.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C11-X1|MT073-C11-X1]] — Few data sets show FX positioning; CLS, a major FX settlement provider, offers heavily anonymized flow data, which the regulator-level Bank of England data far exceeds in granularity.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C15-1|MT073-C15-1]] — The strategic equity-bond split, derived from 10-year capital market assumptions and a correlation matrix, is the allocator's key decision.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C16-3|MT073-C16-3]] — Long-horizon valuation metrics (Shiller PE, 10-year views) are only useful for very long horizons, not for tactical use.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C17-1|MT073-C17-1]] — Mean-variance frameworks were designed for assets, not for strategies that trade those same assets, creating a conceptual problem.

## [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149]]
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C13-5|MT074-C13-5]] — Industry labels all futures-trading products as CTA, so definitional standards may be needed.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C14-2|MT074-C14-2]] — Best-in-class selection per bucket argues for individual manager selection instead of one provider.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C15-3|MT074-C15-3]] — Customization should keep the strategy pure: do not speed up the portfolio or overweight carry on request.

## [[MT075 - fIrlQDvcy_M]]
- [[MT075 - fIrlQDvcy_M#^MT075-C01-5|MT075-C01-5]] — Regime shifts only hurt strategies that omit the regime variable (e.g. rates), so include such inputs rather than blaming the regime.
- [[MT075 - fIrlQDvcy_M#^MT075-C01-6|MT075-C01-6]] — Traditional quants build simple causal models (e.g. pair trading Google and Microsoft), which can fail when the underlying relation changes.
- [[MT075 - fIrlQDvcy_M#^MT075-C02-2|MT075-C02-2]] — The AI should learn which variables matter for a strategy instead of the researcher guessing, including oil even if its relevance is unknown ahead of time.

## [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293]]
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C04-3|MT076-C04-3]] — It is easier to predict the distribution of outcomes (high versus low probability paths) than direction, and betting on those paths is as profitable.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C07-5|MT076-C07-5]] — Time is not linear: event windows matter much more than equal-length periods without events, so forecasts should be tied to the event calendar.

## [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations]]
- [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations#^MT077-C03-X1|MT077-C03-X1]] — A return far above the market's should be judged by the risk taken to earn it, so performance must be evaluated risk-adjusted rather than by raw return.
- [[MT077 - How to Act Rationally During a Financial Crisis with Scott Nations#^MT077-C07-4|MT077-C07-4]] — Since 1896, Dow daily returns show no autocorrelation, so believing recent action predicts the next day is fooling yourself.

## [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment]]
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C01-3|MT078-C01-3]] — Oaktree does not rely on macro forecasting or market timing, because forecasters are not right consistently enough to follow.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C01-4|MT078-C01-4]] — Economists, unlike active managers, never show a track record, so their forecasts should not be hired or followed without one.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C02-2|MT078-C02-2]] — Because of randomness, a good decision can fail and a bad one can succeed, so judge decisions by process, not result; in the short run randomness can produce almost any outcome.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C02-3|MT078-C02-3]] — Success can come from aggression at the right time without skill, which can make one right once or twice; only repeated success over a career demonstrates skill.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C04-1|MT078-C04-1]] — A forecaster should name a price or a date, not both, since valuation signals have unreliable timing.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C06-4|MT078-C06-4]] — Games teach probabilistic thinking, risk-taking, structuring bets and knowing when you hold an edge, which carries over to investing.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C07-3|MT078-C07-3]] — Contrarianism is not mechanical; ask what the consensus thinks, what is wrong with it, and what could expose the error.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C09-4|MT078-C09-4]] — Edge comes from interpreting data and judging where we stand in the cycle, not from having data.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C09-6|MT078-C09-6]] — Because of randomness an investor cannot be right every time (unlike a dentist), so one must be able to stand being wrong.
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C10-1|MT078-C10-1]] — A hit rate of 60-70% makes an investor exceptional, so do not require infallibility.

## [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306]]
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C05-1|MT079-C05-1]] — Do not drop a market from a trend portfolio just because it lost money for years: it may be a diversifier whose future is unknown.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C05-2|MT079-C05-2]] — Dropping short-term interest rate markets near the zero bound would have been wrong because rates went negative; one-sided-market assumptions fail.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C05-4|MT079-C05-4]] — Market selection criteria: two-sided markets with hedgers and speculators; markets with only speculators are a problem.
