- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C02-1|MT120-C02-1]] — Price data is the pure, hard-to-manipulate signal, whereas official economic data can be delayed, manipulated or missing, which weakens economic-trend signals.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C02-X1|MT120-C02-X1]] — A trend strategy built on economic data (such as AQR's economic trend) depends on official releases, so a 40-day gap in US data exposes a weakness, comparable to losing exchange price data for 40 days, even though economic data change slowly.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C06-1|MT120-C06-1]] — Judge strategies primarily by out-of-sample performance and by how stable statistics remain when parameters are permuted, because overfitting the past is easy; then also look at downside/upside capture and Sharpe.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C06-2|MT120-C06-2]] — Combine parameter schools (fixed vs dynamic, recent vs long data, per-market vs common) rather than rely on one view.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C09-X1|MT120-C09-X1]] — Analysis of one-off events such as Liberation Day lacks statistical significance, so they warrant thought about volatility estimation and signal response but not mechanical parameter changes.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C09-X2|MT120-C09-X2]] — Economic trend is more art than rule: it is essentially macro under a different name, so one has to distill which macro strategies connect to trend and how much trend they capture.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C15-X1|MT120-C15-X1]] — A Dunn paper compares alternative investment strategies including trend in crisis periods to test which are true alternatives that diversify in stress.

## [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P]]
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C06-4|MT121-C06-4]] — Customized portfolios are easy to over-optimize, so robustness requires an honest discussion with clients about what is achievable and what pushes the fit too far.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C11-4|MT121-C11-4]] — A Stanford paper on divestment found that excluding stocks from portfolios has not worked as well as investors working with companies to change their practices.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C12-3|MT121-C12-3]] — On a small, rudimentary data set swapping standard equity index futures for ESG counterparts showed no return drag, even a slight pickup.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C12-5|MT121-C12-5]] — ESG ratings and frameworks are not standardized, so it is unclear whether ESG adds value or is mainly an exclusion.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C13-1|MT121-C13-1]] — Environmental factors dominate S and G and, with non-standard analytics, ESG impact cannot be quantified consistently beyond simple exclusions like energy, grains or fossil fuels, so each institution builds its own factor framework.

## [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C01-4|MT122-C01-4]] — Elliott's research process is iterative: build an industry-specific list of questions and answer them through former employees, customers and analysts.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C01-X2|MT122-C01-X2]] — Elliott's ideas come from several sources: people bring them, the Street brings them, and the team ferrets out its own, followed by an iterative question list per industry.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C02-5|MT122-C02-5]] — Success is defined as winning a meaningful share of the requests and the stock price reflecting the added value over time, not just a short pop.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C02-7|MT122-C02-7]] — Singer guesses that value is added in nearly all cases, but only about 70% of cases lead to actual changes in structure, capital structure or directors.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C02-X1|MT122-C02-X1]] — Engagement tactics vary: some campaigns stay under the radar and are disclosed only to the company, others involve public position filings, depending on the rules and strategy, and usually start with a detailed discussion and a long presentation deck.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C06-2|MT122-C06-2]] — Experience adds judgment beyond models and legal-probability estimates, which can be badly wrong in event-driven situations.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C08-1|MT122-C08-1]] — Early specialization gives a narrow skill set poorly equipped for real-world events, so breadth in history, politics and philosophy is valuable for investing.

## [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C02-1|MT123-C02-1]] — Andurand, mainly an energy and metals trader, entered cocoa because an analyst proposed it, and then both dug deep into fundamentals to build a thesis outside his usual markets.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C04-1|MT123-C04-1]] — Even in a less transparent market, specialist consultants survey the main producing regions monthly, giving enough data to forecast next year's crop.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C04-4|MT123-C04-4]] — Judged in real terms, the 1977 cocoa peak equals about $28,000 a ton today, so the nominal 'all-time high' headline overstates how stretched prices are.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C04-5|MT123-C04-5]] — Use historical shortages to see how prices reacted and how long supply took to recover, then adjust for what is different today.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C06-5|MT123-C06-5]] — Cocoa inventory data are partial: US stocks are on exchange but only 20% of European stocks are; port stocks in Ivory Coast and Ghana are visible, and specialist firms estimate the rest with cross-validation.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C06-X1|MT123-C06-X1]] — Andurand screened coffee (large Vietnamese production losses) and judged it interesting but not exciting enough to trade, and had not looked at orange juice, showing he picks only supply shocks big enough to dominate the balance.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C09-1|MT123-C09-1]] — Method for a fair price: model supply and demand year by year at current prices, assess price sensitivity of each side, and treat the price path (not a single price) as the question.

## [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C01-4|MT124-C01-4]] — In a geopolitically driven market, the work is reading how the war and sanctions will evolve, and calls are hard to make with confidence.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C02-4|MT124-C02-4]] — Process: model demand growth versus supply growth to project inventory direction and the price needed to balance the market so storage or inventory limits are not breached.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C03-3|MT124-C03-3]] — Understanding physical inventories, storage and delivery mechanics is essential even for a futures-only trader because physical balance drives price.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C11-5|MT124-C11-5]] — In global GDP deflator terms $150 in 2008 equals about $220 today, so record nominal prices understate real stretch.

## [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C02-6|MT125-C02-6]] — In Andurand's balance model, a 15% cut in residential/commercial gas via 3 degrees C lower thermostats plus a 5% cut in power demand removes the need for Russian gas.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C12-2|MT125-C12-2]] — The IEA estimates each degree lower thermostat saves about 10 bcm (about 5%) of European gas, so 3 degrees is about 15%.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C12-X1|MT125-C12-X1]] — Andurand sizes the thermostat saving from published data: the IEA's estimate of about 10 bcm (5%) per degree and a 2014 study of indoor winter temperatures for the historical baseline.

## [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C03-6|MT127-C03-6]] — July dispersion among trend managers came from asset-class risk weights, market inclusion choices and model speed, with long oil and long stocks positions diverging in outcome.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C09-1|MT127-C09-1]] — AI summarises existing knowledge but cannot easily produce new alpha ideas, so it frees researchers to be creative rather than write Python.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C09-2|MT127-C09-2]] — AI shifts quant research time from implementation (Python, backtest setup) toward ideas, from about 50/50 to 90/10, squeezing junior implementation roles.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C09-3|MT127-C09-3]] — Test researchers by having them critique an AI-generated piece, since AI use is unavoidable; the risk is skill atrophy from one-click answers.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C10-X1|MT127-C10-X1]] — Quant firms have told due-diligence questioners that researchers write their own code and review each other's code; AI code generation will change that answer and shift the control to review of AI-written code.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C11-3|MT127-C11-3]] — Second-order portable-alpha design questions (rebalancing rule, cash buffer) are under-researched, motivating the paper.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C12-X1|MT127-C12-X1]] — The paper's results use SG Trend as the alpha, but the same analysis was run internally on other alphas (such as multi-strategy), with the claim that the cash-buffer and rebalancing conclusions do not depend on trend alone.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C13-5|MT127-C13-5]] — Only one historical path exists, so Monte Carlo is used to simulate severe drawdowns beyond the history.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C14-3|MT127-C14-3]] — Rebalancing the beta component periodically causes tracking error versus the index; to track closely, buy and hold or use tolerance bands.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C15-1|MT127-C15-1]] — Over the long run rebalancing frequency (monthly, quarterly, annual, 5-10% bands) makes little performance difference, but it matters in episodes such as 2008 and COVID.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C15-3|MT127-C15-3]] — Other popular stacked combinations may look great in backtests but hide risks not exposed in recent decades, partly through correlations.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C15-X1|MT127-C15-X1]] — Because long-run results are the same across rebalancing rules, choose the rule by looking at the worst historical paths and picking the one that avoids outcomes the investor could not accept, rather than by average performance.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C16-1|MT127-C16-1]] — The Turtle rules were hand-calculable approximations of later decision and probability theory.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C20-1|MT127-C20-1]] — Test alpha vs the worst 5% of beta returns; trend historically has low to negative correlation to equities in the worst periods.

## [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386]]
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C02-2|MT128-C02-2]] — A small account trades a dynamically optimised subset of 250 futures, matching an idealised large portfolio, so it may hold no position in an instrument despite a strong forecast.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C11-3|MT128-C11-3]] — The 0.7 figure in Rob's book is the ratio of trend-strategy correlation to underlying return correlation, not a fixed book correlation.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C11-4|MT128-C11-4]] — Estimate book correlation from the combined P&L streams over a rolling 6-month window, optionally exponentially weighted; this includes asset and signal correlation.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C12-1|MT128-C12-1]] — Volatility and correlation have different predictability, so estimate them separately and combine them into the covariance matrix.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C12-2|MT128-C12-2]] — Volatility is predictable (R-squared about 0.35 month-on-month), correlation less so (0.2-0.25), and means are unpredictable noise.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C12-3|MT128-C12-3]] — Use about a 6-month window for correlation and about 1 month for volatility, since correlation structure changes more slowly.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C12-X1|MT128-C12-X1]] — Extra trading caused by updating a slow (six-month) correlation estimate is negligible compared with the trading caused by signals, so a time-varying estimate has little cost compared with a fixed assumption.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C13-X1|MT128-C13-X1]] — The Man AHL paper argues that differences between trend managers come mainly from which markets they trade (the market universe), not from better models or faster signals.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C17-1|MT128-C17-1]] — Manager selection depends on investor objective: choose a traditional-futures manager for tail protection and cash needs.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C17-2|MT128-C17-2]] — Systematic finance has always used supervised machine learning in the form of regression and optimisation, blending into unsupervised and neural-network methods.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C18-1|MT128-C18-1]] — AI works best in tightly defined areas such as execution research, where tick data is rich and fits non-linear models; much of fund AI is marketing or blue-sky.

## [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321]]
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C13-X2|MT129-C13-X2]] — Goldman's 3% forecast assumes the economy contracts only about 10% of the time (four quarters in 10 years), so the low number comes from concentration and valuations, not a pessimistic macro scenario.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C14-2|MT129-C14-2]] — Trend followers do not forecast, so they need not be right about forecasts such as Goldman's.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C14-X1|MT129-C14-X1]] — Goldman's earlier 10-year return model, run ten years ago, would have undershot what US equities actually delivered, a caution about such forecasts (their model has since changed).
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C16-3|MT129-C16-3]] — Adding trend to a 60/40 portfolio is not only diversification: trend trades assets already held, so overlap in exposures must be considered in the efficient-frontier analysis.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C16-X2|MT129-C16-X2]] — Systematic managers interviewed are sceptical about machine learning and use it mainly for execution, which contrasts with the multi-asset survey where 90% report using AI.

## [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401]]
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C01-1|MT130-C01-1]] — AI is changing how quant research is done day to day, and the team is in an exploration phase while keeping an underlying process.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C04-3|MT130-C04-3]] — Allocate strategically to what you are solving for rather than chasing what did well last.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C06-X1|MT130-C06-X1]] — Sophisticated QIS investors no longer pick strategies from a menu on a good backtest alone; the industry has matured in how it designs and assesses these vehicles, including capacity checks.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C11-X1|MT130-C11-X1]] — There is no firm line between alternative and conventional markets beyond liquidity, so conclusions about trading alternative markets depend on how the line is drawn.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C12-X2|MT130-C12-X2]] — One year of the broad-universe versus concentrated gap does not settle the choice: adding alternative markets is neither a recipe for disaster nor for outperformance in a single year; the case rests on long-run Sharpe.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C13-2|MT130-C13-2]] — In QIS execution is passive: the bank guarantees reference prices (closing or TWAP) and bears execution risk, so there is no execution alpha for clients.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C13-X2|MT130-C13-X2]] — Execution research has real value, but a QIS provider's boundaries make it an internal risk-management focus, not an externally delivered return.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C15-X1|MT130-C15-X1]] — A single-stock time-series trend portfolio can be decomposed into cross-sectional momentum (e.g. long 60 and short 40 of 100 stocks equals momentum on 40 plus 20 net long beta); the research question is how much of the return comes from market timing versus the cross-sectional premium.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C16-1|MT130-C16-1]] — Comparing long-short strategies (and trend) to the S&P 500 is an incorrect benchmark.

## [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363]]
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C05-1|MT131-C05-1]] — The usual evidence for CTAs slowing is ex-post: correlate stylised bottom-up fast-to-slow strategies with SG Trend Index on rolling 1-2 year windows and see slower ones correlate more recently.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C05-2|MT131-C05-2]] — A change in correlation to a benchmark is necessary but not sufficient to infer a change in signal speed.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C05-3|MT131-C05-3]] — Adding stop-losses, carry, reversion or value signals, or new markets, shifts correlation to a trend benchmark in the same way a speed change would.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C05-4|MT131-C05-4]] — The SG Trend Index is made of realised returns of real, evolving programs, not a simulation of today's algorithms, so speed-by-correlation analysis on it is questionable.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C06-2|MT131-C06-2]] — Changing volatility or covariance estimation or exposure penalties can show up as an apparent change in speed.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C06-3|MT131-C06-3]] — Correlation alone cannot identify a strategy's behaviour: a value investor looks like a contrarian to a momentum trader.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C06-4|MT131-C06-4]] — Conclusions on industry speed depend on market set, signal calculation (EWMA vs simple lookback), so results are fragile.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C07-1|MT131-C07-1]] — A paper maximising correlation to SG Trend Index found a best-fit half-life of about 66 business days over 15 years, which is not slow, so the 'CTAs have slowed' story is overstated.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C07-4|MT131-C07-4]] — Different trend signals (sign of past return, EWMA, crossover) are functional transformations of each other, so effort belongs in portfolio construction and risk management rather than signal search (AQR paper).
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C09-2|MT131-C09-2]] — The SG Trend Index is the 10 biggest managers, shaped by AUM, survivorship and compromises of running billion-dollar programs, so it is not a pure measure of trend.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C09-3|MT131-C09-3]] — Survivorship in the index means it reflects the best surviving managers and sets a higher bar than a plain trend beta.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C10-1|MT131-C10-1]] — Calling the manager index 'trend beta' assumes risk-management and design choices net to zero alpha; otherwise it is beta plus or minus alpha.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C10-2|MT131-C10-2]] — A live manager-based trend benchmark differs from a backtest of today's models because manager composition and design evolve through time.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C11-1|MT131-C11-1]] — A better trend benchmark would be a simple bottom-up trend model as in academic papers, as bank QIS trend indices already do.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C11-2|MT131-C11-2]] — Third-party aggregated QIS trend indices have survivorship bias since banks drop or replace underperforming strategies.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C11-X9|MT131-C11-X9]] — A rules-based (non-manager) trend index could probably be replicated, giving investors an investable true trend benchmark.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C14-3|MT131-C14-3]] — Monte Carlo sampling of 12-52 period sequences shows strategies with short-term downside mitigation (long vol, trend) have similar average arithmetic returns but a compounded return distribution shifted right.

## [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323]]
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C09-4|MT132-C09-4]] — The forecast 10% vol event came in August rather than September, showing timing precision is limited.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C10-X6|MT132-C10-X6]] — A seasonal/political pattern like election years should not be traded alone; combining it with flow and microstructure analysis gives a better forecast.

## [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD]]
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C02-4|MT134-C02-4]] — In extreme years small choices in universe, speed and vol-sizing produce very different results; each year differs for price-based trend.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C03-X1|MT134-C03-X1]] — Nick's year-by-year study ranks trend speeds from 2 to 12 month half-lives across three universe sizes (about 10 markets, a few mid-size, and 80+ markets) for each of 25 years, which is how he finds that the slowest speed and a small universe won in 2023-25.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C04-3|MT134-C04-3]] — Classify CTAs on three dimensions: style (e.g. vol response), timing (speed) and markets; build a portfolio of managers spread across them.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C04-4|MT134-C04-4]] — The Quantica paper concludes what worked before 2022 differs from the last three years, supporting dynamic parameter selection rather than static parameters.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C11-3|MT134-C11-3]] — Replication design choices (lookback, factors) matter little; robustness to switching them gives comfort.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C12-1|MT134-C12-1]] — CTA managers present design choices with exaggerated confidence, inviting questions about why the change was not made earlier.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C12-2|MT134-C12-2]] — Design is clear when tied to the investor objective (liquid high-beta markets for macro factors, alternative markets for inflation/commodity factor, high-frequency for convexity).
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C13-2|MT134-C13-2]] — Design decisions are never obvious; relying on a precedent regime is overfitting, and Winton's decade of reducing trend followed by trend resumption shows behavioural drift.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C13-3|MT134-C13-3]] — Dispersion across trend followers is healthy, but managers must explain their objectives since investors judge all by the same metrics.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C13-X1|MT134-C13-X1]] — Claims that a design decision was obvious come from hindsight; every design involves trade-offs and uncertainty, so the future should be treated as unknown rather than relying on the regime that just worked.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C14-1|MT134-C14-1]] — Different trend objectives (smooth outcome, outlier hunting, crisis alpha) need different metrics, and underperformance should be explained against the objective (no outliers in the period).
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C14-2|MT134-C14-2]] — Design decisions follow the program objective (CTA-like one-stop product vs risk mitigation vs convexity).
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C14-3|MT134-C14-3]] — Dispersion is unhealthy since it blurs what a CTA is and burdens managers to explain.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C15-1|MT134-C15-1]] — Other hedge fund strategies show less dispersion, so factors can describe and replicate them; CTAs mix trend with other embedded strategies.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C15-3|MT134-C15-3]] — A homogeneous, index-like CTA universe is a problem because investors cannot tell managers apart.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C16-2|MT134-C16-2]] — Some dispersion is good; managers should avoid overfitting, address blind spots and match design to client needs.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C16-X1|MT134-C16-X1]] — No one four years ago would have predicted that very slow models on major markets would win for three years, which is why design winners are unforecastable, while allocators look for a manager who says 'I knew that would happen'.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C17-1|MT134-C17-1]] — Statistical confidence in a manager's true Sharpe ratio takes 10+ years of data, yet investors judge on 1-3 years and recency.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C17-2|MT134-C17-2]] — Years of data needed depends on the size of outperformance and the correlation to the benchmark: high correlation shortens the test, obscure CTAs need much longer.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C17-3|MT134-C17-3]] — The 5% significance level is too strict for allocation; 75% confidence is acceptable, allowing shorter evaluation periods.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C17-X2|MT134-C17-X2]] — The data needed depends on the test: showing a manager's return is positive (zero hypothesis) needs less data than showing they beat a benchmark by a stated margin, so allocators should specify the hypothesis before asking how long.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C17-X3|MT134-C17-X3]] — Greater dispersion across the industry makes it harder to tell managers apart statistically because the differences look random, which links dispersion to longer evaluation periods.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C18-1|MT134-C18-1]] — Statistics lag regime change: alternative markets took 10 years to be loved and then 1-2 weak years to be called crowded.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C18-2|MT134-C18-2]] — Manager selection should rest on sound design, operational due diligence, execution, data, and crisis response (Winton's drift from core trend and return).
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C18-3|MT134-C18-3]] — Change or retire a systematic strategy only when its underlying premium has changed, not on statistics; costs are a separate issue.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C18-X1|MT134-C18-X1]] — After three difficult years investors ask how a manager responds to a drawdown while staying true to trend following; the acceptable answers are improvements to execution, data and risk management, not a drift away from the core approach.

## [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16]]
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C05-2|MT135-C05-2]] — A 26-27 market universe covering equities, bonds, commodities and currencies (70-80% of global futures liquidity) replicates the full CTA universe closely because all players trade in proportion to liquidity.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C06-1|MT135-C06-1]] — Bottom-up replication: for each market build time-series trend strategies with lookbacks from 5 to 300 days and regress them on the SG Trend Index (daily since 2000) to pick the weights that best fit.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C06-X1|MT135-C06-X1]] — A grid of time-series momentum lookbacks can span continuous moving-average-crossover trend rules (long averages positive, short negative), so a regression on a lookback grid can mimic them; breakout systems with trailing stops and entry-only vol scaling have far more degrees of freedom and are not spanned.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C07-1|MT135-C07-1]] — The bottom-up replication achieves about 0.8 daily correlation to the SG Trend Index, which is an average of 10 funds with differing styles.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C07-3|MT135-C07-3]] — Fitting trend weights needs 15-20 years of data because a market that produced no P&L in a decade cannot be distinguished from one managers did not trade.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C07-X1|MT135-C07-X1]] — Dispersion among trend managers comes from implementation choices: which markets, which trend speeds, how much risk weight per market and which constraints; a replication fits all these parameters to the index to remove arbitrary dispersion.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C08-1|MT135-C08-1]] — Fitted market weights change by decade (S&P trendless in 2000-10, trendy 2010-20) but sector weights and trend speeds stay stable under cross-validation, and there is no evidence that trend length rose systematically 2000-2020.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C09-2|MT135-C09-2]] — Large trend managers put about 80% of risk in the same ~20 markets, and averaging the fat-tail weights across managers attenuates toward zero, so the beta can be captured with a handful of common markets.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C09-3|MT135-C09-3]] — Top-down replication: run a robust regression of recent index returns on futures markets to find the long/short basket that mimics it, assuming weights hold one day ahead.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C10-1|MT135-C10-1]] — Top-down regression is flexible and can approximate untraded markets (e.g. Swiss franc via gold and euro) and capture manager innovation.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C10-2|MT135-C10-2]] — Top-down weights lag by the 20-40 day lookback (regional bank crisis 2023 shift missed) and cannot see positions in markets with no recent P&L.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C10-3|MT135-C10-3]] — Top-down and bottom-up replicate the trend index equally well, but their tracking errors are weakly correlated, so a 30% top-down / 70% bottom-up blend lowers tracking error.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C10-X1|MT135-C10-X1]] — The top-down regression works but is mechanically untethered from trend following, which is why a mechanistic bottom-up system with almost opposite strengths and weaknesses is added; the two have fairly uncorrelated tracking errors.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C11-1|MT135-C11-1]] — Top-down is kept because managers constantly innovate; it captures idiosyncratic methods the continuous bottom-up misses, even though bottom-up has better average fit.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C11-2|MT135-C11-2]] — Replicating the broader CTA index (with mean reversion, VIX trading, carry) is harder bottom-up; Andrew Beer's CTA fund uses only top-down.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C11-X1|MT135-C11-X1]] — Replication is not divorced from trend following: the 70% bottom-up leg applies true trend signals with parameters fitted to the index, and the 30% top-down leg adds process diversification.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C11-X2|MT135-C11-X2]] — A top-down regression struggles to capture the fast, multi-day mean-reversion models in the broader CTA index because 20-40 daily observations of 20-40 contracts are not enough to fit them, so it ends up capturing mostly the trend part.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C12-1|MT135-C12-1]] — Adding carry, skewness, seasonality and relative-value features improves fit to the CTA index much more than to the trend index, where the gain is marginal.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C12-2|MT135-C12-2]] — Replication ETFs form a cheap benchmark that performance-fee hedge funds must beat over the long run.

## [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367]]
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C04-X1|MT136-C04-X1]] — Strategies and allocation models validated on the post-GFC flow-driven period may behave differently over a longer sample, so evidence from a unique era is a fragile basis for allocation.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C05-4|MT136-C05-4]] — Capital market assumption and asset allocation models fit historical premia (e.g. size factor with data issues in 2000) and extrapolate them, so results are overfit and judgement is layered on top.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C07-X1|MT136-C07-X1]] — Through late September 2025 CTA indices were still weak: BTOP50 (transcribed 'BUP 50') +2.81% in the month and about flat YTD after months negative, SG CTA +3.5% and -3% YTD, SG Trend about +5% and -3% YTD, SG Short-Term Traders +1.5% and -5% YTD, so the short-term sub-style lagged trend.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C08-3|MT136-C08-3]] — Pair trading replication against single managers is costly because replication has historically had about a 300 bps annual cost advantage over hedge fund CTAs, so the short side bleeds, and timing relative outperformance is unpredictable.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C11-1|MT136-C11-1]] — In 2024-2025 the large majority of simple ETF replication products beat the average mutual fund/hedge fund managed futures peer, which should not happen and suggests a structural reason.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C15-X1|MT136-C15-X1]] — Pivotal Path's multistrategy index is built from data supplied by institutions that actually invest in the funds, which Beer says makes it the most accurate record of what investors earned, better than manager-reported databases.

## [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -]]
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C03-X1|MT137-C03-X1]] — The Renaissance International/Institutional Equity fund (equity-market-neutral style) lost 19% in 2022 and has returned only 3.7% since inception, so a -7% year in 2025 is not an outlier and the headline October loss is a journalist's selected number.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C03-X2|MT137-C03-X2]] — A fund with a mandate for low equity correlation should not be expected to rise just because the S&P 500 rises, so its 2025 shortfall must be judged against its mandate rather than the equity index.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C05-1|MT137-C05-1]] — Only about four Bitcoin halvings exist, too few observations for a statistically meaningful halving-cycle model.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C06-1|MT137-C06-1]] — Backtest data frequency should match holding period: fast strategies need tick data, monthly-holding trend systems need none.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C06-2|MT137-C06-2]] — A slow strategy should be insensitive to a one-day execution delay; if it is not, there is a problem. This also removes the need to trade at the close.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C06-3|MT137-C06-3]] — With monthly holding periods, 3-5 years of build data is too short for calibration and yields non-robust results.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C06-4|MT137-C06-4]] — A backtest has two aims, finding future strategies and estimating past performance; selecting the best in-sample strategies destroys the second because performance is selected on the same data.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C06-5|MT137-C06-5]] — Gold-standard backtest: start with many strategies, roll forward and reweight using only past performance, which gives an honest historic result and a final strategy mix.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C07-1|MT137-C07-1]] — In a rolling selection backtest, begin with all strategies equal weighted (flat performance if half are bad) and shift weight to good ones as evidence accrues.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C07-2|MT137-C07-2]] — Multiple testing: picking the top 1% of a thousand strategies selects luck, since flat strategies will produce 10 amazing ones by chance; choosing too few on insufficient data is the main flaw.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C07-3|MT137-C07-3]] — With statistically robust weighting, the best strategies get higher weights but others stay, since a few years of data cannot prove 10 strategies are best.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C07-4|MT137-C07-4]] — Include trading costs in the backtest from the beginning; strategies that do not make money before slippage should not be considered.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C07-X1|MT137-C07-X1]] — Monte Carlo permutation and out-of-sample holdout tests weed out crazy strategies but do not fix the central flaw of selecting a tiny subset of strategies on too little data (multiple testing).
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C08-1|MT137-C08-1]] — With monthly holding, reoptimise strategy weights about annually (AHL used an annual refit); more frequent adds nothing.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C08-2|MT137-C08-2]] — With 55 years of data an extra year adds under 2%, so Carver refits weights about every three years, typically only when other changes are made.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C09-1|MT137-C09-1]] — Trading during the day after the previous close gives performance between trading at the previous close and at the next close, so if backtests at both are close results are robust.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C11-2|MT137-C11-2]] — Trend systems can differentiate speeds between entry and exit signals, and risk management and use of volatility have evolved, without changing the core rules.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C15-2|MT137-C15-2]] — Third-party sentiment indicators or models offered by sell-side are typically in-sample fitted, so be wary and build in-house.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C16-1|MT137-C16-1]] — Volatility scaling by sqrt(time) holds only with zero autocorrelation; negative autocorrelation makes daily vol exceed annualised vol, positive the opposite.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C16-2|MT137-C16-2]] — Weekly ATR should be about sqrt(5) times daily ATR under zero autocorrelation.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C16-3|MT137-C16-3]] — More frequent data gives more accurate volatility estimates, though backtests can run weekly for weekly systems.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C16-4|MT137-C16-4]] — Cheap execution idea: slow momentum signals executed the next day only if price has moved in your favour (buy only after dip) combines slow trend with short-term mean reversion.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C16-X1|MT137-C16-X1]] — Following Andrew Lo's work on hedge fund Sharpe ratios, performance and risk should be estimated from monthly, weekly or daily data because annual returns hide autocorrelation that biases annualised Sharpe and volatility.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C17-X1|MT137-C17-X1]] — The execute-only-on-favourable-move idea delays some trades by more than one day, and it is untested whether the missed trend capture offsets the execution saving; a one-day delay is harmless, longer ones need testing.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C19-3|MT137-C19-3]] — TPA lacks formal definition; a quant formulation would be Markowitz optimisation with utility and constraints.

## [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,]]
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C05-2|MT139-C05-2]] — Both top investors cross-checked every idea with outside people, who else knows or holds a view; this reduces idea error.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C05-3|MT139-C05-3]] — Tiger required analysts to present one idea to add and one to remove each week, forcing preparation and debate.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C08-1|MT139-C08-1]] — Process: about 80% of ideas come from macro analysts, and bottom-up stock analysts pick the efficient securities; technology expertise is also needed.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C08-X1|MT139-C08-X1]] — A personal network of local players and policymakers, built over decades of country trips, lets Citrone get up to speed fast when a long-dormant market (Latin America) becomes interesting; keeping coverage of out-of-favour markets lets him switch long or short quickly.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C09-1|MT139-C09-1]] — Portfolio construction starts from the view on equities, dollar and rates direction, then positions are populated where weakness or strength is.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C12-2|MT139-C12-2]] — Portfolio structure: about 10 themes with 10-15 positions each, 120-150 positions, baskets counted as one position.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C13-1|MT139-C13-1]] — Cross-reference ideas with outside contacts (policymakers, consultants, other managers), as more people in the firm reduce time for outside checking.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C13-X1|MT139-C13-X1]] — Following markets around the clock (European close, Asian open) through a 24-hour trading desk gives Citrone a feel for market direction and some trading edge.

## [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests]]
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C02-X1|MT140-C02-X1]] — A firm's research culture follows its founders: AHL was founded by three physicists and outsiders who took a scientific outside view of markets, whereas many US CTAs came from pit-trading lineage with a more visceral, intuitive market understanding; each approach has strengths and weaknesses.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C03-1|MT140-C03-1]] — Required skills depend on trading frequency: high-frequency needs programmers and data analysts, low-frequency macro needs broader market and economic knowledge.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C04-1|MT140-C04-1]] — Skepticism is the key trait: almost every error comes from overconfidence in backtests, unclean data or forward bias in prices.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C05-1|MT140-C05-1]] — LTCM still had a Sharpe ratio above 3 after its collapse, so Sharpe is not an ideal metric; there are no ideal performance metrics.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C05-3|MT140-C05-3]] — LTCM's failure was the peso problem: data too short to include outliers; any metric including outliers would have shown too much leverage.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C05-5|MT140-C05-5]] — Carver evaluates geometric return via bootstrap and considers a conservative percentile of the distribution rather than the median.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C06-1|MT140-C06-1]] — Explicit fitting done right weights parameter sets by statistical evidence (slightly more for the best) instead of choosing one best parameter, using rolling or expanding windows.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C06-2|MT140-C06-2]] — Implicit fitting: choosing models or removing markets after seeing the backtest uses future performance knowledge, undoing robust explicit fitting.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C07-1|MT140-C07-1]] — Put all model choices into one explicit procedure where weights shift only as evidence accrues, giving a realistic backtest and avoiding choosing a model with hindsight.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C08-1|MT140-C08-1]] — Pure trend following may have stopped working for a decade, but low Sharpe makes this undetectable statistically.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C08-10|MT140-C08-10]] — Normalised momentum applies momentum to a risk-normalised price series, giving more normal distribution and cleaner signal.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C10-1|MT140-C10-1]] — Because he trades slowly with slow decay, Carver adds about one new signal a year or less, with a strike rate of about 20% of ideas researched, and prefers failing to overfitting.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C10-2|MT140-C10-2]] — The main system innovation of the last decade was handling a small account while trading over 200 futures markets, unrelated to finding an edge.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C10-3|MT140-C10-3]] — Crude portfolio construction works as well as sophisticated optimisation techniques.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C11-1|MT140-C11-1]] — At AHL a shared research library avoided redoing earlier work and allowed building on it, with seminars including traders and middle office to catch practical flaws.

## [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)]]
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C02-3|MT141-C02-3]] — Jim Rogers method: look for major secular change and test whether the numbers match the story before acting (research, research, research).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C05-X1|MT141-C05-X1]] — Soros' original process was to test a short list of ideas in open conversation and drop any that someone convincingly argued against, i.e. ideas are vetted by outside challenge rather than held on to.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C05-X3|MT141-C05-X3]] — Having in-house specialists whose country or sector reads can be trusted without fact-checking (e.g. Fraga on Brazil) speeds decisions and lets the team size positions with confidence across markets.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C07-3|MT141-C07-3]] — Restricting a macro investor to long/short equities (at investors' request in 2000) lost asymmetry and hurt results; use the fund's true skill and full instrument set.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C10-3|MT141-C10-3]] — Macro conviction process: lay out fundamentals (current account, valuation versus historical level), give reference points for potential, then add technicals such as exporter positioning and possible cascades.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C10-X1|MT141-C10-X1]] — Conviction in the Abenomics yen trade was built by primary research with a policy architect (Yale economist Hamada) who mapped out how the policy would work, i.e. go to the source of a policy change to check intent and feasibility.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C12-3|MT141-C12-3]] — Knowing future headlines does not give the trade: war in Israel with lower oil, or a 7% deficit with higher rates yet a tech bubble, show that mapping events to prices is unobvious.

## [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King]]
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C03-1|MT142-C03-1]] — A macro trade needs a vision of a different state of the world plus the timing (when, why, how) and the implementation, and it must be mispriced.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C04-X1|MT142-C04-X1]] — The catalyst was spotted through a trusted local contact (a Japanese journalist and policy thinker) who told him that Abe would return on a reflationary platform before it was priced: a local network gives early reads on political catalysts.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C12-3|MT142-C12-3]] — Investing combines quantitative work and a qualitative narrative: build scenarios and then test whether they can be proved.

## [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299]]
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C03-1|MT143-C03-1]] — Market expectations for the 2-year yield a year ahead are always wrong (chart of rate forecasts as filaments around the actual line), so near-term macro predictions are no better than a coin toss.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C03-2|MT143-C03-2]] — Macro models failed to anticipate the 2010s (no fiscal restraint yet no inflation in 2014-15), so people have no good replacement.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C05-2|MT143-C05-2]] — Short-selling alpha seen in selected funds (Einhorn on Lehman, Chanos on Enron) vanished the moment allocators invested, because of selection bias and a halo effect.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C09-1|MT143-C09-1]] — Faster exits and risk reduction in trend following lower drawdowns but add whipsaw risk and probably hurt the Sharpe ratio over time.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C09-2|MT143-C09-2]] — We cannot know the counterfactual of unchanged models over 15 years; his own firm's changes did improve Sharpe.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C09-X1|MT143-C09-X1]] — Trend followers have broadly lengthened their lookback periods, and recent manager innovation has focused on reacting faster on the exit side (risk reduction on more than volatility).
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C09-X2|MT143-C09-X2]] — The common view of trend following Sharpe (about 0.3 as the caption renders it) understates the last ten years of managers' realised Sharpe, which was higher even through the difficult period (caption garbled, treat the exact figure as uncertain).
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C10-1|MT143-C10-1]] — Predicting drawdowns in advance (stop prevention) is hard and expensive; the space's returns would suffer if everyone tried.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C10-2|MT143-C10-2]] — Hedge fund fees take about 80% of alpha.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C10-3|MT143-C10-3]] — Hedge fund indices miss large firms (Citadel, Wellington, sometimes Millennium), so Pivotal Path holdings data is the most complete.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C11-X1|MT143-C11-X1]] — Method point: Pivotal Path's managed-futures category covers 58 funds against 20 in the SG CTA index and its data starts in September 2015, so the 340bp alpha estimate spans roughly nine years including the long winter and is a broader sample than a standard CTA index.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C12-X1|MT143-C12-X1]] — A low-cost replicator can deliver roughly 300-400bp more net return than a 1.5-and-10 fee structure for similar exposure, which is why it risks taking assets from incumbent managers rather than only growing the pie.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C17-2|MT143-C17-2]] — Low fees are not good if the product is worse; a hedge fund at 10 before / 6 after fees beats a cheap fund at 6 before / 3 after.

## [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312]]
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C05-X1|MT144-C05-X1]] — Listener test (read out by the host): macro-momentum signals for growth, inflation, policy, risk sentiment and trade applied to about 100 futures markets reproduced AQR's published results, yet adding them to price trend plus carry lowered the total Sharpe ratio, despite much more complexity and harder data.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C06-2|MT144-C06-2]] — Macro momentum is probably quite correlated with price trend because both rest on the same latent factors, so adding it probably improves Sharpe only modestly.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C06-3|MT144-C06-3]] — Portfolio Sharpe rises by adding either a higher-Sharpe stream or a lower-Sharpe stream with very low or negative correlation; a fall from 1.3 to 1.2 is not a deal-breaker.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C06-4|MT144-C06-4]] — When adding strategies look at correlation rather than Sharpe, because Sharpe ratios are noisier and more dispersed in backtests than correlations.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C06-X1|MT144-C06-X1]] — From running a global macro system at AHL: collecting and analysing macro data is more expensive and time-consuming than price-based trend following and brings its own data problems, which weighs against adding macro signals.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C07-2|MT144-C07-2]] — If correlation is about 0.89 the extra work is not worth it for an individual, although a big fund with staff may still benefit.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C08-1|MT144-C08-1]] — Hierarchical construction: instead of extra multipliers at each level, give higher risk weight to groups with more internal diversification (e.g. more momentum signals than carry signals).
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C08-2|MT144-C08-2]] — A stop loss both sets risk per trade and the holding period, creating a false choice between large risk with long holding and tight risk with premature exits.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C17-2|MT144-C17-2]] — If you can pick winning CTAs, do so rather than index; the index is for those who cannot.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C19-3|MT144-C19-3]] — The SG CTA index has only 20 managers, so it can be undiversified if it misses a big winner.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C19-4|MT144-C19-4]] — An index serves for benchmarking and as long-history proxy in asset allocation, which is institutional thinking.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C19-X1|MT144-C19-X1]] — Disagreement on breadth: the host (inferred from Carver's reply 'one of your things') sees no evidence that trading 250 markets beats 50, whereas Carver is in the more-is-better camp and therefore dislikes replicating with few futures markets.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C20-1|MT144-C20-1]] — Prefers bottom-up replication (replicate positions) over top-down (replicate an index) for statistical reasons.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C20-X1|MT144-C20-X1]] — For non-institutional buyers who just want trend exposure, matching a CTA index closely is not important; an ETF with a trend-following style and reasonable fees serves them, since benchmarking is an institutional preoccupation.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C21-1|MT144-C21-1]] — Labelling a backtest as an index makes it a public track record, which could mislead since managers cannot sell on backtests.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C21-X1|MT144-C21-X1]] — Back-tested indices underlying ETFs are not new (smart-beta indices such as FX carry); the risk is disclosure that sets an index backtest next to the live track record so that it misleads, and he is unsure whether prospectuses show the backtest.

## [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3]]
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C04-3|MT145-C04-3]] — Delta-1 trend following has a Sharpe of about 0.3-0.4; hedged spread trading removes the trend component, which is the most macro one, to raise Sharpe.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C07-X2|MT145-C07-X2]] — Definition used throughout: skew is the gap between upside and downside volatility, and convexity is nonlinear risk in both directions (being long or short options-like payoffs), i.e. an asymmetry in how volatility rises; these describe risk that standard deviation misses.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C10-2|MT145-C10-2]] — Negative skew predicts returns only in the short recent cycle, not over the full cycle, a research trap.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C10-4|MT145-C10-4]] — Modern trend followers choose horizons by data rather than committee, so a drift to longer horizons reflects what data supports.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C10-X3|MT145-C10-X3]] — Index-level critiques (flat skew, style drift) blend diversified CTAs with a small group of pure trend followers, so the analysis should separate the two groups.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C11-2|MT145-C11-2]] — Optimising on the last 15 years (market up 16% a year vs 7% normal) is a short-term optimisation; long-cycle factor exposure is the alternative.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C11-X1|MT145-C11-X1]] — Niels suggests the flat or negative skew of the trend-following index may be an artefact of 20 years of rising equities, so skew should be measured over a much longer horizon.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C13-3|MT145-C13-3]] — Fixed income gave long-term CTAs positive skew; in a large inflation regime that contribution may vanish, so CTA convexity should be tested ex fixed income.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C13-X1|MT145-C13-X1]] — Two short-term systems with the same time frame and the same skew can have opposite results, so horizon and skew do not fully explain performance; she says the firm is researching what separates them.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C13-X2|MT145-C13-X2]] — Market regime shifts are harder to detect than assumed, and vulnerabilities from adaptations to a regime can take years to show up.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C14-5|MT145-C14-5]] — Quant researchers should use long cycles in their data, not only the last 15 years that dominate the available data.

## [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358]]
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C03-3|MT146-C03-3]] — They built a paper-traded CTA replicator from the bottom up (market selection plus a moving average crossover model) to attribute trend-following performance.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C03-4|MT146-C03-4]] — A 20/120-day moving average crossover gave a robust correlation to the SG Trend index, chosen to maximise correlation rather than performance.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C05-3|MT146-C05-3]] — DBi's replication rebalances weekly with about 10 core positions, a short horizon bet on accuracy, while the underlying driver it captures is long-term trend.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C06-1|MT146-C06-1]] — Bottom-up CTA replicators calibrated at one time lose correlation as the industry evolves, with widening dispersion; top-down replication stays adaptive with correlations in the 80s-90s.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C06-2|MT146-C06-2]] — Replication should not bet on a specific trend speed because the optimal one changes with time; a self-adaptive method is needed.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C06-X1|MT146-C06-X1]] — Test of bottom-up trend-model calibration: repeating the model-selection analysis as of 2012, 2009 and 2006 gives different answers about the best speed, so a bottom-up index fitted at one date drifts as the industry evolves (the Mount Lucas managed futures index worked in one period but diverged badly over longer periods).
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C07-1|MT146-C07-1]] — A blend of long and short trend looks like medium-term, which can be painful because it lacks long-term benefits and short-term stress gains: the middle is neither one nor the other.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C07-2|MT146-C07-2]] — Building bottom-up CTA models adds model risk from the many parameters, so inferences from them carry large noise.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C08-X1|MT146-C08-X1]] — Allocators plug SG CTA index data into capital market assumptions and value predictability, so a single manager 20 points off the index breaks the portfolio analysis; this single-manager risk is why they either diversify across managers, use a multi-manager platform, or use broad replication.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C10-1|MT146-C10-1]] — Broad-based, low-cost beta exposure is useful for allocators who cannot do multi-manager due diligence, whereas pension allocators prefer picking four managers.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C10-2|MT146-C10-2]] — Comparing a replication product to a hedge fund index is apples to oranges since the index is net of fees but not investable; a replication-based index has no tracking error to its own product.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C10-X1|MT146-C10-X1]] — Replication monitoring: run many bottom-up trend models and apply the same PCA to them to confirm that the top-down replication is picking up the main themes, accepting periods of divergence.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C11-1|MT146-C11-1]] — Tracking error of a replicator should be measured against the SG CTA index and not against an index built from its own model.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C11-X1|MT146-C11-X1]] — Benchmark choice follows the user's job: a pension head of CTA allocations uses hedge fund data, whereas a wealth manager wanting efficient exposure should build capital market assumptions and benchmark managers on an investable replication-based index, to which the replicator has no tracking error.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C12-X1|MT146-C12-X1]] — Aspect Capital's paper documents the difficult periods across 25 years of the SG Trend index, evidence that the weak 2025 start is not unprecedented and will not be the last such period.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C13-3|MT146-C13-3]] — A single market trend strategy has an IR of 0.3-0.5, but diversification is the only free lunch, so adding markets raises portfolio Sharpe if not highly correlated.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C14-2|MT146-C14-2]] — Over 10-year and rolling 5-year returns, ultra-diversified 300-500 market portfolios have not beaten 70-100 liquid-market portfolios, and no manager has shown evidence apart from backtests.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C16-1|MT146-C16-1]] — Execution should target the same price (open or close) used in the backtest; slippage versus a chosen reference price is partly random.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C16-X1|MT146-C16-X1]] — Slippage and implementation-cost measures depend on the reference price chosen, so a manager can pick a settlement price that flatters results; reported cost figures need scrutiny.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C17-2|MT146-C17-2]] — Marginal diversification from 15-50 to 150 markets is material even if 150 to 151 is not; alternative-market CTAs like Man Evolution and Systematica built strong records.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C17-X1|MT146-C17-X1]] — Design idea: read signals from a broad set (about 150 markets) but implement them through about 15 liquid positions to cut implementation cost while keeping the information.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C17-X2|MT146-C17-X2]] — Periods when trends occur cannot be predicted, which is why the breadth-versus-cost trade-off turns on the predictability of trends and should be settled by evidence.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C18-1|MT146-C18-1]] — To judge a space, ask smart people who chose not to do it; quants say alternative markets do not trend more, but incentives (winning mandates) push complexity.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C18-X1|MT146-C18-X1]] — Settle the breadth debate with 10-40 years of actual returns rather than narratives; managers in alternative markets may still be worth holding because they are different, not necessarily better.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C19-X1|MT146-C19-X1]] — Allocators should demand that a manager state implicit trading costs next to the headline fee (for example 1.5% all-in plus about 400bp of costs) and justify why the extra markets are still worth it.

## [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour]]
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C04-X1|MT147-C04-X1]] — A stock can be decomposed into expected profits plus a discount rate made of the risk-free interest rate and the risk premium for waiting, so one can read from prices what is priced in: here the price move matches the discount-rate change, not an earnings change.

## [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C03-X1|MT148-C03-X1]] — Central-bank distortion contaminates the bond signal and can contaminate the whole-index signal, but relative lead-lag between industries inside the stock market stays clean, so he builds the forecast from the pattern of which industries lead and which lag rather than from index or bond levels.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C03-X2|MT148-C03-X2]] — He claims the bottom-up industry lead-lag approach has beaten the Fed's economic forecasts consistently over 20-30 years (self-reported, not audited).
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C04-X1|MT148-C04-X1]] — A leading-industry signal is credible only when several leading industries (trucking, homebuilders, retail) weaken together; one weak group alone is ignored.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C05-X3|MT148-C05-X3]] — Investing is an art form that changes from cycle to cycle, so rules and instruments that worked in earlier regimes (bonds as the bear-market hedge) must be re-derived each cycle rather than followed mechanically.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C09-X2|MT148-C09-X2]] — Entering before an idea is public gives an asymmetric payoff: if the thesis is wrong the price has not moved so exit costs little, and if right others find the story within weeks and move the price in your favour.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C11-X2|MT148-C11-X2]] — He cross-checks rising stock prices against real-economy anecdotes from small businesses; when firms report poor business while the market rallies, the market is the one more likely to be wrong.

## [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C01-3|MT149-C01-3]] — Druckenmiller says his forecast confidence is lower than usual because of the stimulus shock, Covid momentum, Ukraine and China reopening.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C02-X2|MT149-C02-X2]] — He is far more confident about the direction (hard rather than soft landing) than about the timing of the downturn, so positioning should rest on the direction call while timing is treated as uncertain.

## [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C04-6|MT151-C04-6]] — Invest first, investigate later (Soros's approach): take a meaningful starting position on a concept, then do deeper work.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C07-1|MT151-C07-1]] — Performance is judged against the opportunity set available that year, not an absolute number.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C10-5|MT151-C10-5]] — Machines trade with disciplined process but an intuitive investor using AI as a co-pilot should beat both.
