- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C13-1|MT151-C13-1]] — Analyst and portfolio manager skills differ; being a good analyst does not mean being suited to trigger pulling.

## [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview]]
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C12-X1|MT152-C12-X1]] — As his core price-action-versus-news signal degraded, he plans to adapt by doing more fundamental work; process must change when an old signal stops working.

## [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets]]
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C05-5|MT153-C05-5]] — Algos built on historical models misfire when a regime changes, making them disruptive to those relying on price action.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C06-1|MT153-C06-1]] — Man plus machine (Kasparov) beats pure machines; use machine inputs but understand when signals are real versus algo-driven.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C06-2|MT153-C06-2]] — He allocates small amounts to machine managers as signal providers, treating their alerts as 'under review' for a year or two.

## [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025]]
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C02-3|MT154-C02-3]] — In noisy markets rely first on analysis of facts, then on pattern recognition from experience, since episodes rhyme without being identical.

## [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328]]
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C03-X2|MT155-C03-X2]] — Knightian framing: risk is countable and measurable, uncertainty is subjective and non-countable; in principle subjective probabilities can turn uncertainty into expected returns, but in model building the open question is how to decompose and incorporate the two.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C04-X1|MT155-C04-X1]] — Backtests cannot reveal or measure uncertainty events (they 'occur from left field'), so models that ignore uncertainty look fine historically and come undone when it hits.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C06-3|MT155-C06-3]] — Of top-down and bottom-up replication, bottom-up is better; cheap CTA exposure for ordinary people is what matters.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C07-1|MT155-C07-1]] — Replication lacks a long enough track record across regimes to judge its risk.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C08-1|MT155-C08-1]] — A replicator can infer trend exposure from manager performance data rather than from price data.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C08-2|MT155-C08-2]] — A replicator can only match the underlying CTAs, so if managers change profile (e.g. reverse) the replicator follows them, an existential rather than technical risk.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C13-1|MT155-C13-1]] — The CTA AUM index is distorted by Bridgewater, which is not a CTA; some CTA-like assets sit outside databases.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C14-2|MT155-C14-2]] — Knowing when a market will move is more profitable than knowing why, which favours timing/trend over explanatory narratives.

## [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151]]
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C07-1|MT156-C07-1]] — Separate beta, smart beta/risk premia and alpha explicitly because they need different data, skill and fee levels; market-neutral alpha is the hardest.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C07-2|MT156-C07-2]] — Market-beta-and-factor-neutral alpha measured by information ratio is the hardest return stream to deliver.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C07-X1|MT156-C07-X1]] — BlackRock Systematic's platform rests on Grinold and Kahn's active portfolio management: find an edge and put it into portfolios systematically (the framework behind the fundamental law).
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C07-X2|MT156-C07-X2]] — Systematic firms have long used predictive AI (older machine learning) and now also generative AI; client demand now centers on generative AI, so the label hides which kind of model is doing the work.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C08-2|MT156-C08-2]] — Trend is not good in all environments but is very diversifying in particular ones, so it should be held for diversification.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C09-X1|MT156-C09-X1]] — The pure trend ETF (ISMF) deliberately keeps a simple, expected trend profile, while the multi-strategy ETF (iAlt) is more ambitious and adds momentum and machine-learning elements to modify the payoff profile.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C10-2|MT156-C10-2]] — Fundamental law: IR = skill x sqrt(breadth) x transfer coefficient; momentum has little skill so breadth and risk budgeting drive results.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C10-5|MT156-C10-5]] — Narrow replicator trend ETFs may have outperformed by lucky market selection rather than by systematic edge; commodities add breadth.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C11-X1|MT156-C11-X1]] — Very concentrated trend baskets outperform when they happen to hold the right markets and miss other periods; the goal is consistency of delivering the intended outcome, not luck-driven outperformance.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C12-2|MT156-C12-2]] — Merton-style capital-structure signals link equity and debt value.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C16-X2|MT156-C16-X2]] — Liquid alternatives should be defined by strategy (long/short, momentum or trend as intellectual property) rather than by asset type; trend is an alternative strategy that can deliver uncorrelated return even when trading ordinary assets.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C18-1|MT156-C18-1]] — Cheap trend ETFs (85 bps) compromise market breadth for scalability and may fail to match traditional CTA performance in a stress event.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C18-2|MT156-C18-2]] — In the uncorrelated space dispersion between products is large, so manager selection requires looking under the hood.

## [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421]]
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C03-2|MT157-C03-2]] — Markets and society are complex adaptive systems where small events (an eviction story leading to an election call) cascade unpredictably, which is why trend following responds rather than forecasts.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C05-1|MT157-C05-1]] — (Quoting Nordic report) Portfolios should start from required returns, affordable risks, source of diversification, liquidity and what happens when assumptions fail, rather than from a fixed 60/40 pie.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C07-X1|MT157-C07-X1]] — Strategic asset allocation implicitly assumes more predictability over ten years than over three months, which he calls a little crazy; the rationale is behavioral discipline, not forecasting skill.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C08-3|MT157-C08-3]] — The SG CTA index (20 largest managed futures funds, since January 2000) gives ~27 years of daily net returns to study the space as a whole.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C08-4|MT157-C08-4]] — Use the index rather than single managers because the sector is more predictable than any one fund and shows behaviour across environments.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C09-X1|MT157-C09-X1]] — Crisis alpha is judged only against equity bear markets, because equities drive growth in nearly every portfolio; a commodity decline does not hurt the whole portfolio, so only equity drawdowns count as crises.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C11-3|MT157-C11-3]] — CTA alpha vs S&P 500 was about 330 bp per year over 27 years (high among hedge fund strategies) and is valuable because of the low correlation; alpha is a measure of hidden equity exposure.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C11-X1|MT157-C11-X1]] — Even in the bond bull market, 50-150 bp of alpha versus equities was a win for bonds, and gold's 9% alpha is mostly its recent surge; CTAs' 330 bp is at the top of the range, so alpha comparison favors CTAs.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C13-2|MT157-C13-2]] — The SG CTA index has a 0.34 net Sharpe (cash +275 bp) comparable to equities and bonds (~0.4), rates 0.46, commodities 0.15-0.2; illiquid assets show higher Sharpe by not marking to market.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C13-3|MT157-C13-3]] — The 0.34 Sharpe is after trading costs and 1.4%/14% fees, whereas index Sharpe ratios are free and not investable.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C13-X2|MT157-C13-X2]] — Model guides say crisis alpha plus low correlation is so valuable that an allocator would hold the product even at zero Sharpe, so the positive 0.34 Sharpe is a bonus on top of the diversification case.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C13-X3|MT157-C13-X3]] — Comparing CTAs with hedge fund Sharpe ratios (for example Millennium above 2 for 30 years) is misleading because of selection bias and non-marking; being placed in the hedge fund bucket makes a 0.34 net Sharpe look disappointing.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C14-3|MT157-C14-3]] — Fund dispersion within CTAs is wide and persistence is absent, so differences are mostly modeling luck, making manager selection hard.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C14-X1|MT157-C14-X1]] — Capital-market assumptions are anchored on a long-term cash return that most people got wrong ten years ago, and everything else is built on it, so the output is 'somebody's call' rather than a measurement.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C18-1|MT157-C18-1]] — Managers who have recently done well credit model nuances, but allocators cannot evaluate those nuances and differences are mostly noise.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C18-X1|MT157-C18-X1]] — Due-diligence questionnaires push managers into irrelevant details (such as the exact moving average), and consultants and institutional allocators expect it, so going into the weeds is a necessary evil even if it does not help long-run outcomes.

## [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304]]
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C13-X2|MT158-C13-X2]] — Quant research should attribute performance, asking what is working and what is not, rather than accepting good results; AHL's attribution pointed to diversification across alternative markets as the real determinant post-GFC.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C14-1|MT158-C14-1]] — A Quantica simulation framework showed diversified portfolios pay off most in years when trend following struggles, while concentrated ones win in strong-trend years.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C15-1|MT158-C15-1]] — ML/AI pattern recognition fails in unprecedented events (Covid), so AI-based trend systems did not preserve crisis alpha or zero correlation; AI has not revolutionised trend following yet.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C15-2|MT158-C15-2]] — LLMs help with research documents, code and NLP on balance sheets, but outputs can hallucinate and need checking.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C16-2|MT158-C16-2]] — Capping long-equity exposure in a trend program cuts long-term returns (as seen in 2016 research and Quantica Q2); in 2017, one big long-equity trend, capped trend lagged by 10-20%.

## [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger]]
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C02-1|MT159-C02-1]] — In a complex multivariable system it is hard to know which variables are dependent and an unknown one can flip the whole system, so macro forecasts are unreliable and a correct call may be luck.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C03-X3|MT159-C03-X3]] — Goods-only trade deficit statistics omit services, intellectual property and the foreign earnings of US multinationals, so the headline imbalance used to justify tariffs overstates the problem (relaying Ricardo Hausmann).

## [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301]]
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C03-X1|MT160-C03-X1]] — Even a systematic trader who made an allocation timing mistake concludes that timing is too hard to do by judgement and should be left to the model.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C05-X1|MT160-C05-X1]] — Geometric (compound) return is the right ultimate performance measure, which is why a Sharpe-improving but return-reducing asset can still help once leverage is allowed.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C09-2|MT160-C09-2]] — Computing trend and momentum on a single back-adjusted continuous price series avoids a forecast change at roll, whereas per-contract calculation changes positions at roll for no real reason.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C10-1|MT160-C10-1]] — Using carry of the held contract at roll versus a smoothed continuous series is an immaterial approximation; the EMA smoothing of carry absorbs the change, though seasonal markets like crude and gas are more affected.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C11-X3|MT160-C11-X3]] — In the US system the popular vote does not decide the winner (2016), so even a lead in national polls leaves the presidency near 50/50.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C14-2|MT160-C14-2]] — General large language models give meaningless platitudes on real data analysis tasks, so they are poor tools for evaluating strategy returns.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C15-1|MT160-C15-1]] — Using off-the-shelf LLMs to find predictive patterns in financial data carries high overfitting risk and gives no statistical uncertainty or in-sample/out-of-sample discipline, unlike transparent fitting.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C15-3|MT160-C15-3]] — In an experiment fitting up/down sequence patterns, as pattern length rises from 1 to 10 periods (up to 1,024 patterns) in-sample returns improve until the equity curve is a straight line while out-of-sample performance deteriorates; simple one-lag trend following stays similar in and out of sample.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C17-1|MT160-C17-1]] — Since costs are known and returns are not, between two new managers choose the cheaper one, while established managers with great net returns (Renaissance) keep high fees.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C17-X1|MT160-C17-X1]] — Many managers charge incentive fees without a hurdle, i.e. on total return including cash interest instead of only excess over the risk-free rate, which matters most when rates are high.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C18-1|MT160-C18-1]] — Compare fees after adjusting for volatility target: 2-and-20 on a 25% vol CTA equals 1-and-10 on half the vol, so CTAs look cheap relative to low-vol long/short funds.

## [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357]]
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C10-2|MT163-C10-2]] — The share of CPI based on estimates rose from 9% to 35% by June 2025, so official data may be less objective.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C10-X1|MT163-C10-X1]] — Jim says that during the previous four years employment data were revised repeatedly in the same direction, flattering initial prints and fixed afterwards, so the first release is biased.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C11-1|MT163-C11-1]] — Official payrolls diverged from ADP (job losses) with government jobs filling the gap, so private data sources deserve more weight than government data.

## [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359]]
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C14-1|MT164-C14-1]] — Kelly (AQR/Yale) argues for complex machine-learning models with many factors since prediction, not explanation, matters; traditionalists warn complexity leads to overfitting; simple models reach about 90% of the prediction.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C14-2|MT164-C14-2]] — Causal inference requires only factors with a measurable causal impact, shifting the debate from simple vs complex to causal vs spurious.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C14-X1|MT164-C14-X1]] — A study of fixed-income/equity correlation with about 135 factors finds simple models get about 90% of the prediction; extra factors add little unless machine-learning methods are used, in which case more factors help.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C15-1|MT164-C15-1]] — Model coefficients (e.g. housing models) are unstable over time, so more complexity increases instability and overfitting risk; wisdom lies in simplification.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C15-X1|MT164-C15-X1]] — Complexity is seductive: sophisticated models can be mistaken for better market understanding, and the failure shows only when it is too late.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C16-2|MT164-C16-2]] — Because nobody can predict energy costs, leaders or the Fed, one should follow prices which reveal the future as quickly as possible.

## [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360]]
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C02-2|MT165-C02-2]] — Payrolls are survey-based statistical estimates with falling response rates (CPI imputation rising) and annual benchmark revisions, so revisions are partly mechanical, not necessarily political.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C05-1|MT165-C05-1]] — GDP is mismeasured in a digital, services economy (unpaid self-checkout, influencers); systematic traders do not rely on the data, but other investors do, and GDP, production, income and employment sometimes diverge.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C05-X2|MT165-C05-X2]] — Structural mismeasurement of a digital services economy matters less for month-to-month changes, so cyclical signals built on changes remain usable.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C11-1|MT165-C11-1]] — The WSJ claim that trend following had its worst start since 1998 used an obscure index (Pivotal Path); trend has had only about six down years since 1998 and the late-1990s were positive, so the headline needs context.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C13-2|MT165-C13-2]] — After tough periods hold trend following based on a worldview that markets are inherently unstable and statistics cannot prove it broken; zero correlation means periods of losing while equities rise.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C14-1|MT165-C14-1]] — Long-term performance measures are sensitive: the SG Trend 10-year return went from about +96% (June 2024) to about +30%; 10-year Sharpe 0.1, while the S&P is around 0.8, which drives allocator exits.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C16-1|MT165-C16-1]] — The AQR study backfills trend using the SG Trend index (adjusted), the HFRI Systematic Macro index (discounted 60% for survivorship) in the 1990s, and a hypothetical in-house system (discounted 60%) before 1990.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C16-X1|MT165-C16-X1]] — In AQR's study the liquid diversifier (trend) had a historical Sharpe of 0.44; the forward-looking analysis assumed lower but still supported the same result.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C16-X2|MT165-C16-X2]] — Using managers that lived through the 1970s as a proxy suffers survivorship bias, which is why AQR backfills with discounted indices instead.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C17-X1|MT165-C17-X1]] — AQR's conclusion rests on pessimistic equity return assumptions and relatively optimistic diversifier return assumptions, so the case depends on one's beliefs on expected returns.

## [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361]]
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C06-1|MT166-C06-1]] — Trend followers share systematic, non-predictive rules and participation in sustained moves, but differ in objectives so their design (smoothness, symmetry, upside) differs.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C06-2|MT166-C06-2]] — A given metric (Sharpe) means different things per strategy objective; a high Sharpe may signal an outlier-hunting design is leaving upside on the table.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C07-2|MT166-C07-2]] — Replicator archetype: 10-30 liquid markets, daily volatility targeting, often cross-sectional momentum blended in, goal is low tracking error to the SG CTA index.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C07-3|MT166-C07-3]] — Core diversifier archetype: liquid markets, often cross-sectional momentum, emphasis on smoothness and drawdown control to lift portfolio Sharpe/MAR via diversifying returns.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C07-X5|MT166-C07-X5]] — Homogenised league tables (Sharpe, MAR, drawdown) hide different design objectives, so allocators can end up holding trend strategies whose behaviour in stress differs from what they assumed.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C07-X6|MT166-C07-X6]] — Allocators often treat trend managers as interchangeable because return correlations among them look high, overlooking that differing designs (objectives, speed, breadth) diversify each other in timing and stress behaviour.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C08-3|MT166-C08-3]] — A fifth archetype, 'pure trend', targets best long-term compound return using only trend models, not crisis alpha, diversification or replication; managers must prove this transparently.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C09-1|MT166-C09-1]] — The only universal evaluation metric is a long, validated live track record because it shows survival across regimes; all other metrics must match the archetype's purpose.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C09-2|MT166-C09-2]] — Match metric to archetype: tracking error for replicators, portfolio Sharpe/correlation for core diversifiers, crisis-period CAGR/convexity for crisis offset, skew and top-trade contribution for outlier hunters.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C09-X7|MT166-C09-X7]] — Managers will present whichever metric flatters their own style, so allocators should choose the evaluation metric by the strategy's stated purpose instead of the one shown in marketing material.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C10-1|MT166-C10-1]] — Allocator framework: classify CTA peer group by archetype, decide which archetypes the portfolio needs, then pick the best one or two in each; no single manager delivers all objectives.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C11-2|MT166-C11-2]] — Counterpoint: long-lived trend managers with 20-500 markets show similar rolling 10-year returns but different timing; Niels sees no evidence that 300-500 markets is better and some top outlier-camp funds trade only 20-40.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C12-1|MT166-C12-1]] — Breadth interacts with system design: simple rules work with about 100 markets but would be less functional with fewer, so each design choice cascades.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C15-2|MT166-C15-2]] — Skeptical view: asymmetric long/short rules risk being optimisation; identical rules across markets are arguably more robust, and allocators rarely ask this design question.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C18-1|MT166-C18-1]] — Random 30-market subsets of the 68: only 12% matched the full-book MAR, median MAR was well under 0.5, so 88% chance a narrower universe underperforms purely from market selection luck.

## [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392]]
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C10-1|MT167-C10-1]] — Option volume data (single-day bets) is irrelevant for 3-24 month trends; trend models are built and tested on futures data so should trade futures.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C11-1|MT167-C11-1]] — Man Group paper links objective to universe: choose universe and speed to match whether you want defensiveness or return.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C12-2|MT167-C12-2]] — Crisis protection had been attributed to speed; the paper shifts attention to market universe as the driver of defensiveness.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C13-1|MT167-C13-1]] — The Man paper identifies roughly 900 tradable markets including 60 equity factors; Nick doubts the 60 factors are diversifying since five or six factors explain the equity cross-section.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C14-1|MT167-C14-1]] — Man paper on agentic AI: an agent can rediscover a hidden feature of a breakout signal, but human judgment is needed to frame questions and guard against multiple testing and overfitting; different LLMs give different outcomes.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C14-2|MT167-C14-2]] — LLM answer to the trend-following YTD question was wrong ('mixed') when Jan-Feb 2026 were among the best starts; AI output needs supervision, especially for policyholder money.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C14-X10|MT167-C14-X10]] — Because a systematic pipeline (data, signal, risk, optimiser, liquidity and vol targets) is deterministic, task-specific AI agents narrow in scope but deep in analysis can be run under a supervisor, unlike broad, shallow chatbots.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C15-1|MT167-C15-1]] — Building trend systems rewards simplification; AI tends to expand, which may be incompatible with cleaning signals.

## [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393]]
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C02-X1|MT168-C02-X1]] — The EM (expectation-maximisation) algorithm estimates the parameters of mixed, unlabelled populations by alternating a probabilistic classification of each observation (E step) with re-estimating each distribution (M step) until it converges; it is the calibration tool later used to size the noise, value and trend-trader populations in the flow model.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C03-X2|MT168-C03-X2]] — Over the weeks around a shock, dispersion across CTAs is largely driven by initial positioning luck rather than model quality, so short-window results should not be over-read as skill.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C11-2|MT168-C11-2]] — Discretionary traders watch flows closely while CTAs pay almost none, yet CTAs feature in flow models as one of three player types.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C12-1|MT168-C12-1]] — Paper conclusion: most volatility is noise; the efficient market hypothesis can hold either because price is right (Shiller) or because there is no true price.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C13-1|MT168-C13-1]] — EM-algorithm calibration of the model shows noise is the first order of volatility, value traders second, and trend followers negligible; CTAs create little autocorrelation because footprints are small.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C15-1|MT168-C15-1]] — Critique: assuming a cubic value-trader response (unbounded) is unrealistic because value traders face risk limits too; the paper uses monthly data so cannot inform daily trading.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C16-2|MT168-C16-2]] — Replicating the CTA index at 80% correlation is accepted while S&P replication demands 99%, because CTA constituents are opaque.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C16-X6|MT168-C16-X6]] — Niels argues that although in theory few large markets should replicate a CTA index, in practice replicators' correlation to the index is modest and tracking error substantial.

## [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9]]
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C02-1|MT169-C02-1]] — Trend following was dominant in futures but not applied to stocks; after the 2000-02 tech wreck (market -50%) the firm researched trend on single stocks.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C04-1|MT169-C04-1]] — Stock returns are a power law (Bessembinder, 'capitalism distribution'): 96% of listed firms underperform T-bills, 4% create all wealth, so odds of picking a winner are low and avoiding losers matters.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C05-1|MT169-C05-1]] — Long-only chosen over long-short or market-neutral because investors want to swap equity beta for lower-risk equity beta with limited tracking error.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C05-3|MT169-C05-3]] — Tests on international stocks show the same effect; US focus is a product choice for US advisors.

## [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418]]
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C03-1|MT170-C03-1]] — The book's core figures will be rebuilt annually to 2030 so conclusions are tested against new data.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C05-2|MT170-C05-2]] — Backtests of profit-taking have little statistical power because outliers are rare, one extraordinary trade can flip the verdict, and 125-year data shows bigger events than the 40-year sample.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C05-3|MT170-C05-3]] — Fractal sampling: longer samples reveal bigger drawdowns and bigger positive outliers, so type-II error (missing outliers) is the main risk for an outlier hunter.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C05-X4|MT170-C05-X4]] — A fair test of partial profit-taking holds entries, initial risk and reinvestment rules fixed and compares CAGR, drawdown, MAR and skewness; Rich says it can be done but yields little confidence because so few outlier trades drive the result.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C14-2|MT170-C14-2]] — Non-stationarity: a long successful pattern attracts capital and imitation, so relationships change; a backtest is evidence, not a promise.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C14-3|MT170-C14-3]] — Use historical testing to reject rules that fail their designed behaviour and to reveal turnover, drawdowns and reliance on exceptional trades, but never project one realised average as a constant or ignore unwelcome evidence.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C15-2|MT170-C15-2]] — Backtest purpose: verify rules produce intended behaviour (turnover, drawdowns, failed trades, exceptional winners), not locate future outliers; keep rules mechanical and revisit design in research, not daily.

## [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397]]
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C08-2|MT171-C08-2]] — Systematic models should include the incentives of the dominant actor (the US administration) rather than treat prices as the free outcome of independent supply and demand.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C16-X3|MT171-C16-X3]] — Investors focus on the first-order effect of a technology and under-weight the system's reflexive second-order reaction (political, fiscal, social), so forecasts that follow only the technology's direct effect (for example AI deflation) are biased.

## [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep]]
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C01-X1|MT172-C01-X1]] — Charts are a compact cross-asset summary of what has happened and also work as feedback on one's own views, since price action tells you when you are right or wrong.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C01-X2|MT172-C01-X2]] — When charting a group through an ETF, pick the proxy with the most even weighting (SOXX for semiconductors) so that one stock such as Nvidia does not dominate the group chart; different ETFs for the same group give different pictures.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C03-3|MT172-C03-3]] — A qualitative cross-sectional review of 100 large-cap charts quickly reveals market breadth and which stocks are leading or lagging.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C03-X1|MT172-C03-X1]] — Caveat on bearish chart reads: Sohn's earlier bearish views on Meta (2022, down 60-70%) were wrong because the stock recovered, so a weak chart shows relative weakness now and is not a forecast that the stock cannot recover.
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C11-2|MT172-C11-2]] — Cap-weighted consumer discretionary is dominated by Amazon and Tesla, so equal-weight discretionary (in the bottom decile) is the better gauge of the consumer and of K-shaped weakness; test sector ideas on equal weight.

## [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302]]
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C03-1|MT173-C03-1]] — Reversion built as a cross-sectional strategy and trend as time-series tap different principal components, adding diversification beyond the signal difference.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C03-3|MT173-C03-3]] — Select markets for diversification and cost, not for past performance contribution, as that is data mining (cocoa was the worst contributor until it was not).
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C08-3|MT173-C08-3]] — Quantica finds capping equity exposure helps in downturns but costs more in long-run upside, so run trend unconstrained.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C09-2|MT173-C09-2]] — Quantica tested three equity-constrained variants: no equities, equities short-only, and short S&P futures hedging ex-ante beta to zero.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C09-X1|MT173-C09-X1]] — Quantica's method splits the roughly 97 quarters since 2000 into the worst 16% (below minus one sigma), the best 16% (above plus one sigma) and the normal middle, then compares equity and trend returns in each regime.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C09-X2|MT173-C09-X2]] — Caveat on the crisis beta/alpha split: a quarterly regression assumes beta stays constant within the quarter, which is not true for a trend program that changes positions, so the split is descriptive rather than a precise attribution.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C10-2|MT173-C10-2]] — Neutralising beta with short S&P futures dilutes the trend profile since it shorts rising markets.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C11-1|MT173-C11-1]] — Constraining portfolio beta can flip longs to shorts or increase negatively correlated bond longs, thus increasing exposure to a joint equity and bond selloff (inflation spike).
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C11-X1|MT173-C11-X1]] — Capping equity exposure forces a design decision the paper does not address: either redeploy the freed risk elsewhere (which can add concentration) or accept lower volatility; Dunne notes the capped variants might have been levered up to compensate, so part of their lower long-run return is simply lower risk.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C11-X2|MT173-C11-X2]] — Neutralising equity beta with S&P futures alone ignores the equity beta carried by non-equity positions (commodities, bonds and others), so the way beta is neutralised changes the results; Baltas agrees.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C13-2|MT173-C13-2]] — Goyal and Jegadeesh show time-series momentum is cross-sectional momentum plus time-varying net long investment, whereas a cross-sectional strategy is market-neutral.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C15-2|MT173-C15-2]] — Equity carry from the futures curve is a risk-neutral expectation of dividends, distinct from historical dividend yield which is more like value; commodity carry needs seasonality adjustment.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C15-3|MT173-C15-3]] — A carry z-score standardises today's carry against its own history (1-5 year mean and volatility), making carry relative to normal, like cheap versus its own past in equities.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C17-2|MT173-C17-2]] — Time-series carry gets implicit benefit from trend rather than carry itself (e.g. oil in contango while falling), so carry should be designed to minimise spot contribution when run alongside trend.
- [[MT173 - The Negative Crisis Beta and CTA's Hidden Market Timing Skills - Systematic Investor 302#^MT173-C17-X1|MT173-C17-X1]] — Beta-neutralising a time-series carry portfolio imports cross-sectional logic: if all bonds are long because of positive carry and beta to bonds must be zero, the optimiser shorts half of them, so the result is a mix of cross-sectional and time-series carry and can short assets with a positive signal.

## [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410]]
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C03-1|MT174-C03-1]] — AI is mainly used for research (collecting, synthesising information, coding) with human in the loop; open-ended factual queries can be confidently wrong, so outputs require expert checking.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C03-2|MT174-C03-2]] — A simple AI agent can collect CTA index performance and top stories each morning and flag relevant research papers, though numbers are sometimes wrong when websites lag.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C09-X1|MT174-C09-X1]] — The Run One River report tabulates by year which trend model, which speed and which structure (an integrated trend program versus a portfolio of asset-class trends) did best, implying leadership rotates between years, so a speed or structure should not be chosen from one year's ranking.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C11-2|MT174-C11-2]] — Risk model factors (such as countries) explain risk but are not necessarily rewarded; risk models reduce dimensionality.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C14-1|MT174-C14-1]] — Ignoring thematic clusters in the covariance matrix biases risk estimates and misallocates risk; a theme-aware risk model (Lee) is better and applies beyond stocks to cross-asset trend portfolios.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C15-1|MT174-C15-1]] — The narrative-momentum paper builds point-in-time data from 13.3 million articles since about 2013 into 347 narratives, with robustness checks on narrative definitions.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C15-X1|MT174-C15-X1]] — Price data is objective and recorded for decades, which makes trend research easy to test; thematic strategies lack an equivalent, since themes appear and vanish and it is unclear what the underlying data is or how to back-test them without look-ahead.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C16-1|MT174-C16-1]] — Look-ahead in narrative backtests is analogous to the factor zoo; using 30-60 evergreen topics (trade wars, stagflation, capex) reduces the problem since new themes correlate with them.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C16-X1|MT174-C16-X1]] — Sceptical view: simple sector grouping captures most of the clustering that thematic work tries to model, and thematic investing may partly be a way for Wall Street to get investors excited, since energy or healthcare companies already react similarly.
- [[MT174 - The Next Evolution of Trend Following ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.410#^MT174-C17-X1|MT174-C17-X1]] — The price path is not necessarily the best predictor of the underlying signal, only the most observable and validated one; the macro regime, the price path and thematics are different transformations of the same underlying signal (denoising the truth), and in a prolonged growth regime they all point the same way (buy equities) and correlate.

## [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21]]
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C04-1|MT175-C04-1]] — Gresham trades about 150 markets and turned over roughly a quarter of the list in 2025 (44 dropped, 31 added), selecting on market structure rather than backtests.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C04-2|MT175-C04-2]] — Choosing markets by backtest Sharpe is data mining and 'the key to future misery'; instead evaluate who trades the market, where on the curve, and its correlation to mainstream commodities.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C04-3|MT175-C04-3]] — 'Alternativeness' means low correlation to mainstream commodities (for example the Bloomberg Commodity Index), not low liquidity or barriers to entry; a low-liquidity oil spread correlated with crude is just costly crude exposure.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C06-2|MT175-C06-2]] — European carbon had negative trend Sharpe over its 8 years of history in 2016 but was added on structural criteria and became one of the best performers, an example of selecting on structure not backtest.
- [[MT175 - The Overlooked Markets Creating Better Trend Opportunities - Open Interest - Ep.21#^MT175-C11-X1|MT175-C11-X1]] — The budgeted, passive execution is not discretionary alpha: the desk never trades against the model or on its own views, it only chooses timing within bounds as the market moves towards or away, and the rule could be written as an execution algorithm.

## [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313]]
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C14-4|MT176-C14-4]] — With alternatives assumed uncorrelated to equities and bonds correlated at 0.3, a 10% volatility optimizer favours alternatives over bonds in a stock-bond-alts mix, but the result depends on those assumptions.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C14-X1|MT176-C14-X1]] — Asness's argument that herding has made the crowd less independent, hence markets less efficient, is qualitative with no supporting data, so it should be treated as a hypothesis.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C14-X2|MT176-C14-X2]] — In Asness's 10%-volatility optimisation, a stock-bond-only portfolio comes out very close to 60/40; the departure from 60/40 arises only once an alternative with zero equity correlation is allowed.
- [[MT176 - The Paradox of High-Volatility Alternatives - Systematic Investor 313#^MT176-C16-1|MT176-C16-1]] — Sharpe ratio is a portfolio-level metric and misleads when applied to single strategies; maximum drawdown relative to volatility should also be examined, where some volatile CTAs have much smaller drawdowns than their volatility suggests.

## [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139]]
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C01-1|MT177-C01-1]] — Winton's priorities are original research for new alpha sources and improvement of existing ones, plus hyper-vigilant risk management that monitors many possible scenarios.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C02-2|MT177-C02-2]] — Winton's philosophy is to approach markets with an open mind and use empirical scientific research, rejecting the efficient market hypothesis as a foundation, because good predictions are possible with hard work.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C02-3|MT177-C02-3]] — Avoiding data-mining false positives requires shared statistical techniques for testing many hypotheses simultaneously and sequentially, which also drives hiring and training.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C02-4|MT177-C02-4]] — Process must prevent researchers from privately overfitting many ideas and presenting only the subset that looked good in a backtest.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C03-1|MT177-C03-1]] — Backtest statistics give confidence in Sharpe and low correlation, and an economic rationale with testable intermediate relationships adds confidence; absence of a rationale lowers confidence rather than killing the idea.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C04-X1|MT177-C04-X1]] — Trend following should be judged by the improvement in total-portfolio Sharpe it delivers, not by its stand-alone Sharpe or a 'crisis alpha' mandate, because Sharpe was not created for single-strategy evaluation (reacting to the AQR paper on trend mandates).
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C06-2|MT177-C06-2]] — Labels like crisis alpha mislead because trend only sometimes works in crises; describe its response in different environments instead.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C07-X1|MT177-C07-X1]] — The non-trend quarter of the diversified CTA is a mixture of purely statistical signals, economic-rationale signals and non-trend technical signals, some faster than trend, with new signals added each year.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C08-3|MT177-C08-3]] — Winton finds academic journals no longer at the forefront of alpha research and instead does original research, sometimes finding ideas from Chinese commodity markets that work globally.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C09-X1|MT177-C09-X1]] — Research has two streams: finding new alpha sources (new ideas or data inputs) and maintaining existing ones by reviewing trend speed, signal construction and volatility models.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C10-1|MT177-C10-1]] — Switching trend speed with the regime is an implicit call on policy (e.g. Fed intervention), which systematic managers should not make; discretionary macro managers do that.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C10-2|MT177-C10-2]] — Machine learning lacks enough independent events for slow-trading strategies because few examples of crashes exist, but works for faster intraday strategies with many observations.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C11-1|MT177-C11-1]] — Slow-horizon research is an observational science (cannot rerun a crash) while fast execution research is experimental and can iterate, so machine learning suits the latter.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C11-X1|MT177-C11-X1]] — Claiming that a systematic process is fully automated hides that someone must still choose the parameters or the algorithm that chooses them (an infinite regress), so firms should deliberately decide at which level humans take responsibility.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C12-1|MT177-C12-1]] — Humans should own early design decisions such as trend speed and asset allocation (made with analysis) rather than defer to an optimizer, preserving understanding of the portfolio.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C12-2|MT177-C12-2]] — Regression-based CTA replication (about 95bp fee) is still a single-manager risk; Winton disagrees that it mitigates manager risk.
- [[MT177 - The Secrets of Quantitative Investing - ft. Simon Judes - Top Traders Unplugged 139#^MT177-C14-X1|MT177-C14-X1]] — Counterpoint to the more-markets argument: a 50-60 market classical portfolio already includes diversified commodities, and going from 100 to 200 markets mostly adds correlated financial markets unless the extras are genuinely alternative (e.g. Chinese futures).

## [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403]]
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C01-1|MT178-C01-1]] — Efficient Capital's process mitigates behavioural biases (performance chasing, hindsight) with a tested thesis per manager, recorded decisions and data on where they have an edge; they have no skill predicting which trend manager will lead.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C09-1|MT178-C09-1]] — CTAs seek approximately right solutions and well-thought-out decisions rather than precise but wrong complex formulas.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C09-X1|MT178-C09-X1]] — The backtest-overfitting criticism made of QIS products applies equally to replication strategies, multi-manager portfolios built on backtests and individual CTAs, so what differentiates them is out-of-sample evidence and accountability, not the existence of a backtest.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C10-1|MT178-C10-1]] — Bank QIS strategies are more prone to overfitting than managed products because the issuer does not stake reputation on one strategy and offers many backtested ones.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C10-2|MT178-C10-2]] — Mean-variance optimisation depends on forecasts of returns and covariances, so out-of-sample results are poor and weights unstable; equal risk allocation with tweaks for clusters is more promising.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C11-1|MT178-C11-1]] — The 1/N paper shows estimation error makes complex portfolio choice fail; focus on what can be estimated (correlations a little, volatility reasonably) and use with caution.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C13-3|MT178-C13-3]] — Geopolitical risk is non-stationary (jumps), so the study used the biggest increases and decreases of the index to sort months and examine asset returns.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C15-X2|MT178-C15-X2]] — Crisis-alpha and drawdown analyses depend on the time horizon: at monthly frequency trend cannot show gains from slow-building events like the tech bubble, so the geopolitical result only describes shocks that move prices within a month.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C18-X1|MT178-C18-X1]] — In the host's internal research, basic trend models with short lookback periods end up losing money over time, so short-term trend following is not profitable in a pure sense.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C19-1|MT178-C19-1]] — Managers more different from long-term trend (short-term, diversifying) have higher mortality, so good managers are hard to find and diversifying strategies hardest, though portfolios benefit when used.
- [[MT178 - The Strategy That Thrives When Markets Panic - Systematic Investor - Ep.403#^MT178-C19-X1|MT178-C19-X1]] — Short-term CTAs are not only running short-term trend: many use pattern recognition and must keep innovating because signals decay quickly, so their results are not a clean test of short-term trend.

## [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19]]
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C05-1|MT179-C05-1]] — Ed Seykota's style is computer-assisted technical discretionary pattern-based trading, not algorithmic trend following; he knows system strengths and weaknesses from backtests but uses computers for data display and cleaning.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C06-X1|MT179-C06-X1]] — Seykota believed technical discretionary pattern trading is more profitable over the long run than algorithmic systematic trading, although Drew never adopted it for his investors.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C08-X1|MT179-C08-X1]] — Drew admits he never verified that hedgers actually shifted at the points his patterns flagged; the hedger-shift hypothesis was a better design basis for entry timing than guessing when a trend begins, so the edge was inferred rather than observed.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C09-1|MT179-C09-1]] — Systematic trading involves discretion at design (choice of rules) and none afterwards; the same timing algorithms from 1984 were used until the fund closed in 2023, without overriding.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C12-1|MT179-C12-1]] — The original timing algorithm proved robust for 40 years after extensive testing of alternatives, which Drew programmed himself so he could ask complex portfolio-level questions.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C14-2|MT179-C14-2]] — Portfolios need rules for adding new markets (e.g. heating oil before crude futures) and removing dying ones (potato futures), including how long to wait before trusting a new market.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C14-X1|MT179-C14-X1]] — Systematic strategies should be tested on the longest possible history (Drew obtained 1970s Board of Trade charts and typed in the data by hand) to see markets and regimes such as 1970s potato trends.
- [[MT179 - The Trader Who Never Spoke...Until Now - Open Interest - Ep.19#^MT179-C16-1|MT179-C16-1]] — Design questions (e.g. half weights for correlated petroleum markets versus full weight on first signal) should be tested over rolling 5- and 10-year blocks to see if the answer is robust.

## [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298]]
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C05-X1|MT180-C05-X1]] — Recommended managed-futures allocation sizes vary widely: a Man paper suggested about 40% of a portfolio while the replication-ETF issuer targets model portfolios at only 3-5%, so ETF-based adoption assumes small weights.
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C10-1|MT180-C10-1]] — Replication-based ETFs have lower manager-specific risk and look like industry beta but are backward-looking, slower, and inherit managers' risk management; direct implementation uses actual signals and risk rules but reflects one manager's parameters and can deviate from the index.
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C10-X1|MT180-C10-X1]] — A hybrid between index replication and direct implementation is sensible: it keeps CTA insight in signals and risk management while tracking the industry beta that ETF buyers expect (as with an S&P 500 ETF).
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C11-1|MT180-C11-1]] — A replicator carries single-replicator model risk; the correlation to the target may be less than expected.
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C12-1|MT180-C12-1]] — Flat-fee ETFs still require research focused on extended markets and differentiated techniques that justify performance fees, because the mutual fund shift pushed managers toward scale versus return.
- [[MT180 - The Trend Following ETF Revolution - Systematic Investor 298#^MT180-C13-X2|MT180-C13-X2]] — Using AI to hunt for the best new signal (for example to trade cocoa) will not improve outcomes for the marginal investor; AI's value is helping investors access better portfolios and make better decisions.

## [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351]]
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C02-2|MT181-C02-2]] — AQR now uses machine learning to find market patterns even when the reason is unclear, which may help returns but makes poor performance hard to explain to investors; trend followers prefer knowing why they are long or short.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C07-X1|MT181-C07-X1]] — Platforms such as QuantConnect and StrategyQuant X offer built-in robustness testing and link the backtest workflow to live broker execution (e.g. Interactive Brokers), narrowing the gap between backtest and live trading for retail trend followers.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C08-3|MT181-C08-3]] — Unadjusted stitched futures contracts contain artificial jumps at each roll that create false signals, so continuous back-adjusted series are required and should be built by the researcher.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C09-1|MT181-C09-1]] — The Panama (difference) method keeps the current contract price and subtracts each roll gap from all earlier history, so the history must be re-adjusted at every new roll and early prices can turn negative in backwardation.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C09-2|MT181-C09-2]] — Define one consistent roll trigger (open interest, volume or calendar) and observe the price gap at the roll to compute the adjustment.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C09-3|MT181-C09-3]] — CSI Data is named as a low-cost, clean futures data source with user-selectable roll and adjustment methods.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C09-X1|MT181-C09-X1]] — Some backtest engines cannot handle the negative historical prices that Panama back-adjustment can create, in which case another adjustment method must be chosen, although most modern engines accept negative prices.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C09-X2|MT181-C09-X2]] — Under Panama adjustment contango markets have their historical prices raised (and backwardated ones lowered), so only the current front contract shows the true live price while all earlier history is shifted.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C10-1|MT181-C10-1]] — Because back-adjustment shifts historical price levels, avoid rules based on absolute price thresholds, long lookback percent returns, mean reversion to long averages and fixed dollar stops; use indicators agnostic to adjustment bias.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C10-2|MT181-C10-2]] — Backtest roll logic must match live roll logic, otherwise backtest and live results diverge.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C10-3|MT181-C10-3]] — Futures backtests suffer survivorship bias when delisted contracts and dead symbols are omitted, so use data including them.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C10-4|MT181-C10-4]] — Data granularity must match execution: with only daily open-high-low-close data, rules should act only on those points and not infer positions between them.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C11-1|MT181-C11-1]] — A backtest should stress test models and find where they break, not estimate future returns; aim for robustness across regimes rather than curve fitting to one regime or market.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C11-2|MT181-C11-2]] — For new micro/nano contracts with about three years of data, extend history with a proxy: the standard contract on the same exchange with similar price structure (scaled for tick size and tick value), and backtest on 20-40 years of that.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C12-1|MT181-C12-1]] — Alternative to proxies: overlap-based or normalization-based splicing of two similar series; try proxies first.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C13-X1|MT181-C13-X1]] — The capital growth path from $200k to $400k to $1m used to illustrate when to add markets is hypothetical; actual performance and size are unknowable in advance, and the point is only that more assets allow more markets and systems.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C14-1|MT181-C14-1]] — Large language models predict plausible text, not causal relationships, so strategies they propose look polished but lack robustness under stress and overfit.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C14-2|MT181-C14-2]] — An LLM asked for a crude oil system returned a forum-favourite SMA crossover with RSI and ATR filters, not based on oil's volatility regime, seasonality or term structure, so it only works because it is tuned to past conditions.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C14-X1|MT181-C14-X1]] — LLMs produce fluent, authoritative answers that can be false (even inventing books and citations) and claim to have read material they have not, because they lack verifiable truth-checking and symbolic reasoning, so every output used in strategy work must be validated.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C14-X2|MT181-C14-X2]] — LLMs suit language, coding and pattern recognition, whereas causal reasoning needs other kinds of AI or mathematical models, so the type of AI should be matched to the task rather than treating AI as one universal fix.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C15-1|MT181-C15-1]] — Auto-generating hundreds of strategies with AI and backtesting them is mass data mining that produces false positives which die in live trading unless regime validation and structural filters are applied.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C15-2|MT181-C15-2]] — Feeding historical charts to an LLM and asking what entry would have worked creates hindsight bias; rules trained on known outcomes fail when uncertainty is real.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C15-3|MT181-C15-3]] — LLMs are useful for debugging syntax and porting code, but cannot judge whether a system is conceptually sound, for example whether exit logic destroys convexity; human validation with causal logic is needed.
- [[MT181 - The Two Tribes of Trend - Systematic Investor - Ep.351#^MT181-C15-X1|MT181-C15-X1]] — Prompts are the control point for LLM use: embedding the researcher's own causal logic in the prompt steers the model's pattern recognition toward a structurally sound answer, whereas blind use yields the overfitting and hindsight problems described.

## [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398]]
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C04-2|MT182-C04-2]] — Study 'Mimicking Finance': AI agents replicate about 70% of portfolio-manager behaviour, but performance falls below the best managers, who are more dynamic and rule-rich and hard to mimic.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C04-3|MT182-C04-3]] — Replicating trend followers with AI is unnecessary when daily positions of trend replication ETFs are public.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C04-X1|MT182-C04-X1]] — Reverse-engineering trend programs from their behaviour is not new (brokers have tried for four decades); what is new is the use of AI to do it.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C04-X2|MT182-C04-X2]] — Whether extra model complexity (AI agents able to include nonlinearities) actually improves replication of trend following over simpler replication is an open empirical question that must be tested against the simple replicator.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C04-X3|MT182-C04-X3]] — Comparing a replicator's returns with broad Morningstar-type trend or systematic indices is not the right benchmark; the comparison should be with like-for-like funds.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C05-1|MT182-C05-1]] — Trend replication struggles with nonlinear, threshold-based signals (breakouts: dormant until a move passes a threshold) while moving-average strategies are easy to replicate.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C05-3|MT182-C05-3]] — A 0.75-0.8 correlation between a replicator and trend managers does not prove replication works; the replicator has shown more downside volatility and drawdown than the equivalent fund and has not beaten the best managers.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C05-4|MT182-C05-4]] — Correlation is a poor guide to replication quality: a replicator can be highly correlated yet have a negative beta to the managers it targets.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C05-X1|MT182-C05-X1]] — Replication products have, over their short history, kept pace with trend indices and sometimes beaten them, which the speaker concedes while arguing they have not beaten the best managers.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C06-1|MT182-C06-1]] — The alpha component not captured by simple factors cannot be replicated and is what investors pay fees for.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C07-X1|MT182-C07-X1]] — Saying a market is not discounting an event is itself a directional opinion, not evidence, so the claim that equities have not priced the Middle East shock needs a test rather than assertion.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C11-3|MT182-C11-3]] — A comparable study of 14-16 alternative strategies since 2000, the start of the trend index, has been updated and will be published in a weekly email.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C13-1|MT182-C13-1]] — NLP sentiment from earnings calls (bag of words) is profitable in equities, but how to use it in a trend following model is not yet researched.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C13-2|MT182-C13-2]] — Managers who stuck to price-only trend following have done very well across decades of change, and added context (sentiment, economic trend) is not clearly better.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C13-3|MT182-C13-3]] — Multi-agent LLM systems that ensemble technical, fundamental and sentiment agents show better Sharpe ratios than single-agent models.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C13-X1|MT182-C13-X1]] — Besides NLP sentiment, large language models are the second main AI use in quant research, converting non-numerical information into trading signals that could add context to models.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C13-X2|MT182-C13-X2]] — Sentiment and similar context should already be reflected in prices, which are the only hard truth, so price-only trend following remains potent and extra context is not obviously better.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C14-1|MT182-C14-1]] — Blending trend with other strategies in one manager resembles a multi-agent ensemble; the open question is whether to buy pure strategies and blend them or buy the bundle.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C14-2|MT182-C14-2]] — The narrative that using more inputs improves trend following is easy but may add confusion (strong GDP data while markets fall); a narrow price-only approach continues to work.
- [[MT182 - The VUCA World Is Here - Systematic Investor - Ep.398#^MT182-C15-2|MT182-C15-2]] — Deep-momentum research on bimodality finds past winners split into continuing winners and big losers, offering an explanation of momentum crashes.

## [[MT183 - The Volatility Apocalypse - Systematic Investor 303]]
- [[MT183 - The Volatility Apocalypse - Systematic Investor 303#^MT183-C08-X1|MT183-C08-X1]] — Short-term flow indicators must be monitored because they steer the path that eventually breaks and determines long-term outcomes; ignoring them while watching only macro news misses the big picture.
- [[MT183 - The Volatility Apocalypse - Systematic Investor 303#^MT183-C10-X2|MT183-C10-X2]] — The multi-step scenario path (moves one to five) is the most likely path but conditional: it must be re-measured as market characteristics change rather than followed as a fixed forecast.

## [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)]]
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C01-1|MT184-C01-1]] — A strategic leader must do four tasks: get the big ideas right, communicate them, oversee implementation, and refine the strategy repeatedly; Petraeus judges Zelensky to have done this well and Putin poorly, which explains Ukraine taking the initiative.
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C14-X3|MT184-C14-X3]] — A top-down process: first accept the big regime shift (geopolitics now circumscribes economics), then apply it to specific relationships, regions, countries, industries and finally individual investments.
- [[MT184 - The War in Ukraine, Relations With China & Wider Geopolitics (With General David Petraeus)#^MT184-C17-X1|MT184-C17-X1]] — Kodak is the example of failing the fourth strategic-leadership task: it held about 2,000 digital-photography patents but did not make digital the big idea, while Netflix's Reed Hastings is cited as a model strategic leader.

## [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -]]
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C03-3|MT185-C03-3]] — After working with Niederhoffer, the speaker added mean-reversion strategies in the 1990s to offset the expected decay of opening range breakout; the decay took 8-10 more years and is glacial.
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C03-4|MT185-C03-4]] — Replacement reference points for the open include the previous close and overnight movement, previous-day highs and lows and the 24-hour session range; they work, though less strongly than the old opening range.
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C05-1|MT185-C05-1]] — Old portfolios do not work as well as current ones, and the firm changes 10% or more of its portfolio every year because markets evolve; without changes the 5-10 year old portfolio would have gone out of business.
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C07-1|MT185-C07-1]] — Charts show what is going on; the firm quantifies visual observations to detect market changes, and the human mind still extracts information from charts better than a computer, so we still must tell the computer what to do.
- [[MT185 - Toby Crabel on Vanishing Edges, AI Limits, and Future of Markets - Top Traders Unplugged -#^MT185-C09-2|MT185-C09-2]] — Portfolio of six uncorrelated mean-reversion and six momentum general strategies with balanced weight; four of the top five performers that year were the generalized ones even though optimized strategies looked better on paper.
