- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C14-4|MT215-C14-4]] — Possible reasons for CTA Sharpe ratios far below a simple model's: short-term models exit before moves, and risk controls cut positions.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C15-1|MT215-C15-1]] — CTA indices include the biggest managers and their blowups, but omit smaller managers who do well, so may understate best-in-class trend; institutions also pay lower fees than those embedded in the index.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C15-X1|MT215-C15-X1]] — Looking back 10 to 15 years (after system upgrades), the larger, well-established trend managers show somewhat higher Sharpe ratios than the headline SG Trend or CTA index figures that the media quote, so the index mixture understates best-in-class trend.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C15-X2|MT215-C15-X2]] — Beer's simple trend replication has outperformed every large CTA but one (ISAM) since its mid-2016 start, and ranks third against current index constituents, evidence that large managers add little over a simple signal.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C16-1|MT215-C16-1]] — No hidden pool of large, high-Sharpe trend managers exists; efficiency among large managers means a simple replication gets competitive rank.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C16-2|MT215-C16-2]] — ISAM, which is complex and trades hundreds of markets, has been hardest to beat, a counterexample to simplicity being better.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C16-X1|MT215-C16-X1]] — Ranking against the current index constituents carries selection bias because managers drop out and are added and firms switch which fund represents them, so index and peer comparisons must account for membership changes.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C17-1|MT215-C17-1]] — Plotting Sharpe ratios of hedge funds against track record length shows the maximum falls with length: none over 1 at 10 years and about 0.65 at 15-20 years.
- [[MT215 - Why Most Investors Fail at Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.404#^MT215-C17-2|MT215-C17-2]] — A good single strategy sustains a Sharpe ratio of about 0.3-0.4.

## [[MT216 - Why Smart Investors Are Switching to Trend Following - Systematic Investor 334]]
- [[MT216 - Why Smart Investors Are Switching to Trend Following - Systematic Investor 334#^MT216-C07-2|MT216-C07-2]] — Trend following cannot be timed; the case for it is as a permanent component.
- [[MT216 - Why Smart Investors Are Switching to Trend Following - Systematic Investor 334#^MT216-C12-X1|MT216-C12-X1]] — If value signals stop working it may be the definition of value that is outdated (intangibles, brand, a different type of measure) rather than valuation itself being uninformative, so the response is to update the measure before dropping the idea.

## [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405]]
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C05-1|MT218-C05-1]] — Sharpe ratio is the main metric for adding, sizing or removing a strategy; drop one only on very strong statistical evidence of a structural break.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C05-2|MT218-C05-2]] — Drawdown is a poor evaluation statistic: ignoring autocorrelation it grows with history length and falls with Sharpe, so it adds no information beyond Sharpe.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C05-3|MT218-C05-3]] — Looking at path risk and loss clustering for each individual strategy component overcomplicates and risks overfitting; it can matter at the whole-portfolio level.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C06-1|MT218-C06-1]] — Skew estimates are statistically unreliable, so use skew only as a classification device with a cap on the negative-skew bucket, not as a formal utility with Sharpe.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C06-2|MT218-C06-2]] — The factor zoo has hundreds of factors but only a handful of independent drivers, so true diversification is less than the number of factors suggests.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C06-X1|MT218-C06-X1]] — Cross-sectional momentum is a relative factor (winners against losers) whereas trend following is an absolute, time-series factor, so the two should not be treated as the same source of return when counting factors.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C12-2|MT218-C12-2]] — Timing entry or exit of trend following by recent drawdowns does not work: return autocorrelation is too weak to predict returns after drawdowns.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C13-X1|MT218-C13-X1]] — Faced with a pattern such as sector leadership rotating by decade, a quant should first ask why it happens and whether other explanations fit, and only then ask whether it can be exploited (e.g. shifting weight to the hot asset class).
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C14-2|MT218-C14-2]] — A continuous (trend-strength scaled) signal already tilts toward asset classes with stronger trends, so extra performance chasing of asset classes is unnecessary and does not add return.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C14-3|MT218-C14-3]] — Publishing null results is a sign of good research; Quantica found tactical asset-class chasing does not add value.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C16-2|MT218-C16-2]] — AI is fine for idea generation but ideas must be tested in a robust process away from the AI, because the process matters more than the maths.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C16-X1|MT218-C16-X1]] — AI-written code and AI-run backtests cost more in checking and rewriting than they save, so AI is used for ideas and small snippets and never trusted to run a strategy backtest.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C17-1|MT218-C17-1]] — Any idea, from AI or data mining, is conceived with hindsight, so a backtest process must handle look-ahead contamination robustly.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C17-2|MT218-C17-2]] — The output of backtesting should be a probability that a strategy works translating into a portfolio weight (outstanding idea 10-15%, weak idea about 0.01%), not a yes/no decision.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C18-1|MT218-C18-1]] — Due diligence on AI-driven strategies should ask whether models are internal or external and whether trained before a date with a pure out-of-sample test; public AI models may contain forward information.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C18-X1|MT218-C18-X1]] — Due diligence on a CTA should focus on the research process from idea to live trading (steps, checks and balances, robustness, peer review), which is the same whether the idea came from an AI, a junior researcher or data mining, because every idea is formed with forward information and the AI's own evaluation of itself should not be trusted.
- [[MT218 - Why Trend Following Is Harder Than It Looks - Systematic Investor - Ep.405#^MT218-C19-1|MT218-C19-1]] — Drawdown studies need a definition because strictly one is in drawdown almost always; across 50 years there are only 10-20 episodes, so conclusions across assets are statistically weak.

## [[MT219 - Why Trend Following Might Outlast the AI Arms Race - Mark Rzepczynski - Systematic Investo]]
- [[MT219 - Why Trend Following Might Outlast the AI Arms Race - Mark Rzepczynski - Systematic Investo#^MT219-C03-2|MT219-C03-2]] — Markets that long frustrate trend followers (cocoa shorts) later give big trends, so time in the market matters more than timing trend following.
- [[MT219 - Why Trend Following Might Outlast the AI Arms Race - Mark Rzepczynski - Systematic Investo#^MT219-C08-1|MT219-C08-1]] — Crisis alpha studies in the trend industry start with the tech bubble and ignore 1980s-90s upheavals (Plaza Accord, S&L, 1987 crash, ERM, Tequila, Asia 1998), which were also volatile and relevant to trend following.

## [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365]]
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C04-2|MT220-C04-2]] — Return dispersion among trend managers is explained by design choices (speed, allocation, alternative markets, carry), so dispersion is the fingerprint of each system (Man AHL 'Dynamics of dispersion').
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C05-3|MT220-C05-3]] — Index dispersion arises from specific constituents (one outlier in 2009; two discretionary managers in 2014 later removed), so understand makeup before concluding about manager risk or replication.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C06-1|MT220-C06-1]] — CTA indices are not investable and manager style drifts over time, so study aspects (speed, markets) of managers rather than rely on index behavior.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C06-2|MT220-C06-2]] — Dispersion between managers follows mathematically from differences in volatility and correlation among their returns, so theoretical dispersion can be estimated.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C06-X1|MT220-C06-X1]] — The Man AHL method builds many hypothetical trend managers with different designs and aggregates them over time, which allows studying design differences across environments without cherry-picking the best backtest version.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C07-1|MT220-C07-1]] — Replicated indices give different Sharpe (about 0.75 backtest vs 0.36 actual) because longer lookbacks did better historically and managers differ, so focus on relative and conditional differences.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C08-X1|MT220-C08-X1]] — Random-signal experiments (alongside channel breakout versus moving average signals) separate how much return dispersion comes from asset allocation and position sizing versus from the entry signals themselves.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C09-2|MT220-C09-2]] — Create style factors for trend (speed, market size, correlated markets) to regress manager returns and explain relative performance as with equity style factors.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C09-X1|MT220-C09-X1]] — An investor holding two or three CTAs who trails the index by 400-500bp should not treat it as a sign of poor selection: gaps of that size arise by chance between return streams with these correlations.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C10-1|MT220-C10-1]] — A Mahalanobis-distance turbulence metric gives a point-in-time measure of movement across managers, decomposable into magnitude surprise and correlation surprise, without needing a window of data.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C12-2|MT220-C12-2]] — A trend follower should include all assets because an asset that never trended (metals, palladium, copper) can start trending.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C13-X1|MT220-C13-X1]] — To avoid over-reading a short period (managers in alternative markets only have about 10 years of data), Quantica measures Sharpe per individual market and adjusts for the period, then reasons from portfolio theory about correlation, instead of judging by which sectors won recently.
- [[MT220 - Why Trend Following Never Looks the Same Twice - Systematic Investor - Ep.365#^MT220-C14-2|MT220-C14-2]] — Systematic macro did better than trend around Liberation Day, motivating research on economic trends versus technical trends.

## [[MT221 - Why Trend Following Still Works in Tough Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.381]]
- [[MT221 - Why Trend Following Still Works in Tough Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.381#^MT221-C02-X1|MT221-C02-X1]] — A CTA manager uses AI cautiously: it helps with coding, summarising client discussions and the team's own thinking, and as a test of message clarity (an AI misreading a presentation shows where the message is unclear), but it is not presented as a source of alpha.
- [[MT221 - Why Trend Following Still Works in Tough Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.381#^MT221-C05-2|MT221-C05-2]] — A stable, pre-committed process does not remove drawdowns but prevents panic and rule changes made in response to recent outcomes, which is a main investor mistake.
- [[MT221 - Why Trend Following Still Works in Tough Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.381#^MT221-C06-2|MT221-C06-2]] — A trend replication model left unchanged for ten years gives a track record that is fully consistent; the best outcome for an allocator is that the manager never needs changes, because it is hard to know whether changes help going forward.
- [[MT221 - Why Trend Following Still Works in Tough Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.381#^MT221-C07-1|MT221-C07-1]] — Recent CTA underperformance is explained by fewer outsized trends (compression), not by model breakdown; diagnose by measuring the Sharpe extracted from markets that trended at a given underlying Sharpe versus history, which is roughly unchanged.
- [[MT221 - Why Trend Following Still Works in Tough Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.381#^MT221-C08-2|MT221-C08-2]] — Drawdowns are a prompt to review for blind spots but not a reason to change the model, especially when the underperformance is explained by repeated V-shaped reversals (yen, Liberation Day).
- [[MT221 - Why Trend Following Still Works in Tough Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.381#^MT221-C09-X2|MT221-C09-X2]] — A replication or benchmark model need not minimise tracking error to the CTA index, since outperformance matters; high tracking error in a given year does not by itself show the model works or fails.

## [[MT222 - Why Trend Following Works - Systematic Investor - Ep.391]]
- [[MT222 - Why Trend Following Works - Systematic Investor - Ep.391#^MT222-C04-2|MT222-C04-2]] — Trend followers need not decide whether a spike is a fundamental repricing or a positioning overshoot; they stay positioned to follow the confirmed trend.
- [[MT222 - Why Trend Following Works - Systematic Investor - Ep.391#^MT222-C06-4|MT222-C06-4]] — The Fractals of Finance research tests whether trend patterns are a structural property of markets using 68 futures markets across 8 classes with daily data from 1984.
- [[MT222 - Why Trend Following Works - Systematic Investor - Ep.391#^MT222-C10-X2|MT222-C10-X2]] — Volatility expansion can be harvested two ways: buying cheap volatility and selling it on the spike (the Dave Dredge approach) or mechanically via trend following with dynamic position sizing; the difference lies in how position size is treated as vol rises.

## [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373]]
- [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373#^MT223-C03-2|MT223-C03-2]] — Supply and demand elasticities are hard to estimate because shocks hit both curves, giving a biased estimate unless an instrument isolates one side.
- [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373#^MT223-C04-1|MT223-C04-1]] — Gabaix-Koijen granular instrumental variable uses shifts in each holder's share of demand (e.g. between pension funds, mutual funds, ETFs) as idiosyncratic shocks to identify market elasticity without bias.
- [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373#^MT223-C07-1|MT223-C07-1]] — The paper uses slow quarterly data (flow of funds, Morningstar fund flows, 13F, roughly 2000-2021), so one quarter is the horizon and the multiplier cannot be estimated over time.
- [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373#^MT223-C10-2|MT223-C10-2]] — Man AHL paper (Otto Van Hemert, May 2021, The Best Strategies for Inflationary Times) predicted inflation-period performance before 2022 spike and its backtest goes back to 1925.
- [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373#^MT223-C14-1|MT223-C14-1]] — QIS (bank quant index) market of $300-600bn is opaque, making it hard to assess; hedge fund managers must justify their added value against QIS trend products.
- [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373#^MT223-C15-1|MT223-C15-1]] — A study of ~200 alternative-beta (QIS) products found an average 60% Sharpe haircut from pre-launch backtest to live, even for carry and trend, so a backtest Sharpe of 1 delivers about 0.4; a 50% haircut is appropriate before allocating.
- [[MT223 - Why Trend Thrives When Inflation Returns - Systematic Investor - Ep.373#^MT223-C15-2|MT223-C15-2]] — In trend following the signal is a commodity worth about 0.2 Sharpe per market; value comes from portfolio construction, risk management and execution.

## [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411]]
- [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411#^MT224-C04-X1|MT224-C04-X1]] — Observed return statistics are only the residue of a deeper, constraint-contingent process with many interacting variables, so inference from the distribution of outcomes cannot recover the process or forecast the outcome with fidelity.
- [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411#^MT224-C07-2|MT224-C07-2]] — Agent-based model with only random traders gives Gaussian returns with no fat tails, memory or volatility clustering.
- [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411#^MT224-C11-1|MT224-C11-1]] — Reshuffling 40 years of identical returns left Sharpe, Sortino, VaR and standard deviation unchanged but terminal wealth ranged from a 30% decline to an 84% decline, so order-dependent path matters and these metrics miss it.
- [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411#^MT224-C14-2|MT224-C14-2]] — Niels argues the real trend-following edge is risk management and position sizing rather than breakout signals.
- [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411#^MT224-C15-2|MT224-C15-2]] — His tests show simple equal-risk fractional sizing outperforms Kelly-based sizing once regime changes and estimation noise are included, because noise often exceeds the signal; LTCM exceeded the peak assumptions and went to ruin.
- [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411#^MT224-C18-2|MT224-C18-2]] — Two portfolios with identical Sharpe 1.5 (one concentrated Kelly bet, one wide book of small loosely related positions) differ in failure modes; Sharpe discards construction, so architecture and dependencies must be assessed.
- [[MT224 - Why the Best Portfolios Are Built to Be Wrong - Systematic Investor - Ep. 411#^MT224-C28-2|MT224-C28-2]] — Risk is everything not in your backtest.

## [[MT226 - Why the Old Rules of Diversification Are Changing - Allocator - Ep.38]]
- [[MT226 - Why the Old Rules of Diversification Are Changing - Allocator - Ep.38#^MT226-C06-1|MT226-C06-1]] — Because AI forecasts are highly uncertain, policymakers and investors should watch real-time data to see whether the productivity handoff is happening rather than rely on forecasts.
- [[MT226 - Why the Old Rules of Diversification Are Changing - Allocator - Ep.38#^MT226-C06-4|MT226-C06-4]] — Technology-driven productivity is disinflationary (1990s analogy) but could raise or lower the real neutral rate; neither level nor direction of r-star is reliably estimable.
- [[MT226 - Why the Old Rules of Diversification Are Changing - Allocator - Ep.38#^MT226-C10-1|MT226-C10-1]] — Multi-strategy pooling of many alpha streams raises breadth and so, by the fundamental law of active management, information ratio.
- [[MT226 - Why the Old Rules of Diversification Are Changing - Allocator - Ep.38#^MT226-C15-1|MT226-C15-1]] — Systematic teams have used NLP and machine learning for alpha signals and portfolio construction for over a decade.
- [[MT226 - Why the Old Rules of Diversification Are Changing - Allocator - Ep.38#^MT226-C15-2|MT226-C15-2]] — AI tools should increase analyst coverage and idea velocity on the fundamental side, raising breadth, which with forecast quality drives active results.
- [[MT226 - Why the Old Rules of Diversification Are Changing - Allocator - Ep.38#^MT226-C15-X1|MT226-C15-X1]] — AI research engines that draw on the firm's full set of proprietary data improve the quality of the information mosaic and the decision on a single stock, in addition to widening the number of names an analyst can cover.

## [[MT227 - Yield Storm Ahead- Preparing for a New Era - Systematic Investor 330]]
- [[MT227 - Yield Storm Ahead- Preparing for a New Era - Systematic Investor 330#^MT227-C04-4|MT227-C04-4]] — The CBOE SKEW index is a poor measure of skew; a delta-based (probabilistic) measure is better than a moneyness-based one.
